Revisiting the Quantum Geometry of Torus-fibered Calabi-Yau Threefolds
Boris Pioline, Thorsten Schimannek

Abstract
About ten years ago, Katz, Klemm and Huang conjectured that topological string amplitudes on compact, elliptically fibered Calabi-Yau threefolds at fixed base degree could be expressed in terms of meromorphic Jacobi forms for S ​ L ​ ( 2 , ℤ ) SL(2,\mathbb{Z}) , giving access to Gromov-Witten invariants at arbitrary genus. This was later generalized to torus-fibered CY threefolds with N N -sections, where topological string amplitudes are conjecturally governed by meromorphic Jacobi forms under the congruence subgroup Γ 1 ​ ( N ) \Gamma_{1}(N) . In this work, we show that these modularity properties follow from (and are equivalent to) the wave-function property of the topological string partition function Z top Z_{\rm top} under a relative conifold monodromy, implementing a particular Fourier-Mukai transformation on the derived category of coherent sheaves. In particular, we introduce a variant of Z top Z_{\rm top} which is both holomorphic and modular covariant. Under the same relative conifold monodromy, the generating series of genus 0 Gopakumar-Vafa invariants at fixed base degree is mapped to the generating series of rank 0 Donaldson-Thomas indices counting D4-D2-D0-brane bound states wrapped on the torus fiber. We show that the quasimodularity of the generating series of GV invariants matches the expected mock-modular behavior of the generating series of D4-D2-D0 indices, despite having different multi-cover contributions. We analyze and tabulate a large number of CY threefolds fibered over del Pezzo surfaces, with an N N -section for N ≤ 5 N\leq 5 , including several new examples beyond the realm of toric geometry.



1
Introduction

Type II strings compactified on a Calabi-Yau threefold
X
provide a tractable yet extremely rich arena to investigate non-perturbative aspects of string theory, with profound connections to many topics in mathematics including algebraic and symplectic geometry. Topological string theory, obtained by a suitable twist (of A or B-type) of the superstring worldsheet theory
[
1
,
2
]
, encodes a particular set of protected couplings in the low energy effective action
[
3
]
. Remarkably, it also determines (at least in principle) the full spectrum of BPS states
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
.
Mathematically, the topological A-model counts holomorphic curves of arbitrary genus in
X
(more precisely, computes their Gromov-Witten invariants), while
the topological B-model does not yet have a first principle mathematical formulation beyond genus 0 and 1 (where it reduces to variation of Hodge structure and analytic Ray-Singer torsion, respectively;
see
[
7
,
8
]
for attempts to define the B-model at all genera).
Both models are related by mirror symmetry, and computable by localization methods when
X
is toric (hence non-compact)
[
9
,
10
]
.

When
X
is a smooth, compact CY threefold, the only currently available method for computing topological string amplitudes at higher genus, that does not rely on the existence of a fibration structure, is to exploit the holomorphic anomaly equations
[
2
]
.
These have to be supplemented by certain boundary conditions in order to fix the holomorphic ambiguity arising at each genus. Since the number of ambiguities grows faster than the currently known boundary conditions (including
Castelnuovo bounds at large volume and gap vanishing conditions at conifold points), this puts a bound on the maximal computable genus, e.g. 53 for the benchmark case of the quintic threefold
\mathds{P}^{4}[5]
[
11
]
. Recently, this upper bound was pushed up further for the quintic threefold and other one-modulus hypergeometric
models
[
12
,
13
]
, by exploiting relations between Gromov-Witten (GW) invariants and rank 0 Donaldson-Thomas invariants associated to the derived category of coherent sheaves
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}\Coh(X)

[
5
,
6
]
, and enforcing the (mock) modularity of the corresponding generating series of D4-D2-D0 BPS
indices predicted by S-duality
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
. Unfortunately, the infinite set of additional constraints gained in this way still grows slower than the number of holomorphic ambiguities. Moreover, despite overwhelming evidence the modularity properties are still conjectural (see however
[
17
]
for some recent progress).

In the case of CY threefolds with K3 or genus one fibrations, one may obtain further boundary conditions by exploiting the modularity associated with the fiber. For example, for CY threefolds fibered by lattice-polarized K3 surfaces, the generating series of vertical (i.e. zero base degree) Gromov-Witten invariants are known to transform as vector-valued modular forms, being closely related to the Noether-Lefschetz invariants of the fibration, and can be determined to arbitrary genus
[
18
,
19
]
. Similar modularity constraints apply at non-zero base degree, although they are currently only understood at genus 0 and become increasingly more complicated when the
degree increases
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
. Moreover, vertical D4-D2-D0
invariants are determined by the same Noether-Lefschetz invariants
[
23
]
, and hence also transform as vector-valued modular forms, establishing the S-duality predictions in this case.

In this work, we study the case of smooth CY threefolds fibered by genus one curves
1
1

1

We reserve the terminology
elliptic fibration
for a genus one fibration with a section. A general genus-one fibered CY threefold need only admit a multisection of degree
N\geq 1
. We will say that a geometry exhibits a genus one fibration with an
N
-section if there is no
N^{\prime}
-section with
N^{\prime}<N
.
. In this case, it was observed in
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
that generating series of GW invariants with fixed base degree and genus transform as quasi-holomorphic modular forms under
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
(in the case of elliptic fibrations), and satisfy holomorphic (or modular) anomaly equations similar to
[
2
]
. Based on this structure, and the duality with F-theory
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
, it was conjectured in
[
30
]
that the topological string partition function at fixed base degree (but arbitrary genus) can be expressed in terms of meromorphic Jacobi forms, where the topological string coupling plays the role of the elliptic parameter. Since the ring of Jacobi forms is finitely generated, this opens the way to determine the topological amplitude at fixed base degree and arbitrary genus, provided the order of the poles in
the elliptic parameter can be controlled. While the elliptic transformation property has by now been rigorously proven for reduced divisor classes in general elliptic fibrations
[
31
]
, and the holomorphic anomaly equations proven in some special cases
[
32
]
, the modular transformation property remains conjectural. In
[
33
]
these conjectures were extended to the case of genus one fibrations with an
N
-section, where the modular group is restricted to the congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{1}(N)\subset SL(2,\mathds{Z})
defined in (
A
).
It was further observed in
[
34
]
that the topological string partition functions of two smooth genus one fibered Calabi-Yau threefolds with a
5
-section that share the same Jacobian fibration transform into each other under an Atkin-Lehner involution.
More generally, it was proposed that the topological string partition functions of different torus fibrations that share the same Jacobian fibration transform as vector valued Jacobi forms under the full modular group, after taking into account the presence of flat but topologically non-trivial B-fields that non-commutatively resolve certain singularities
[
35
]
, see also
[
36
,
37
,
38
,
39
]
.
This structure has been further clarified and generalized in
[
40
]
.
Physically, the vector valued modularity can be understood after relating the topological string partition function to the twisted-twined elliptic genera of non-critical strings in the six-dimensional F-theory compactification that is associated to the fibration
[
35
,
41
]
.

Our first main result is to derive the holomorphic anomaly equations and modular transformation rules for smooth genus one fibrations
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}B
over a generalized del Pezzo surface
B
with
h^{1,1}(X)=h^{1,1}(B)+1
(in particular no fibral divisors), from the wave function property of the topological string partition function
[
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
46
]
. Recall that the holomorphic anomaly equations of
[
2
]
can be interpreted
[
42
]
as the statement that
the full, non-holomorphic topological string partition function
\Psi_{\rm BCOV}(t^{a},\bar{t}^{a},\lambda,x^{a})
can be viewed as the overlap of a background-independent wave function
|\Psi_{\rm top}\rangle
, living in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}\sim L^{2}(\mathds{C}^{b_{2}(X)+1})
associated to the quantization of the even cohomology of
X
(or middle cohomology of the mirror
\widehat{X}
, depending whether one is interested in the
A
or
B
-model) against a family of coherent states
{}_{t,\bar{t}}\langle\lambda,x^{a}|

parametrized by the background moduli
t^{a}
specifying the complexified Kähler or complex structure, respectively. Under a monodromy around the discriminant locus in moduli space, both the state
|\Psi_{\rm top}\rangle
and the coherent states
{}_{t,\bar{t}}\langle\lambda,x^{a}|
transform according to the metaplectic representation of
Sp(2b_{2}(X)+2,\mathds{Z})=Sp(b_{3}(\widehat{X}),\mathds{Z})
, such that their overlap is invariant. In the limit
\bar{t}^{a}\to-\mathrm{i}\infty
keeping
t^{a}
fixed,
\Psi_{\rm BCOV}(t^{a},\bar{t}^{a},\lambda,x^{a})
reduces to the usual topological string partition function
Z_{\rm top}(t^{a},\lambda)
.
This interpretation was used in
[
44
]
to elucidate the modular properties of topological string amplitudes on local CY manifolds.

In the case of a smooth, compact genus-one fibered CY threefold
X
with an
N
-section, the homology class of the generic fiber is
N
-divisible.
It is therefore natural to introduce a complexified Kähler parameter
T
such that
NT
is the complexified volume of the torus fiber.
There is a natural monodromy
U
, the so called relative conifold monodromy, which acts on this parameter as
T\mapsto T/(1+NT)
, and on the derived category
D^{b}\Coh(X)
by a Fourier-Mukai transformation with kernel given by the ideal sheaf of the relative diagonal
[
47
,
48
,
49
,
33
]
.
2
2

2

The usual conifold monodromy, around the locus where the central charge of the 6-brane vanishes, corresponds to the Fourier-Mukai transformation with kernel given by the ideal sheaf of the diagonal
\Delta\subset X\times X

[
50
]
. The
relative
conifold mondromy arises around the component of the discriminant (in the stringy Kähler moduli space of the Calabi-Yau) where the central charge of a 2-brane wrapping the torus fiber vanishes and the Fourier-Mukai kernel is the ideal sheaf of the relative diagonal
\Delta_{B}\subset X\times_{B}X
. For a gentle introduction we refer to
[
33
, Section 3.2-3.3]
.

For
N=1
, this can be combined with large volume monodromies
T\mapsto T+1
to obtain another monodromy
S
acting as
T\mapsto-1/T
, corresponding to a double T-duality with respect to the genus-one fiber
[
51
,
52
,
53
]
, but in general
S
is not a symmetry, rather it maps
X
to another element in the Tate-Shafarevitch group
[
54
,
35
,
40
]
. Assuming that
Z_{\rm top}(t^{a},\lambda)
transforms under
U
according to the metaplectic representation, we shall derive the Jacobi transformation property of the topological string partition function at fixed base degree under
T\mapsto T/(1+NT)
, recovering the predictions of
[
30
,
33
]
. Conversely, this may be taken as evidence that the topological string partition function (an object whose mathematical definition remains obscure) does transform according to the metaplectic representation under monodromies. A key step in the derivation of the Jacobi properties is a new representation of the topological wave function
Z_{\rm mod}(t^{a},\lambda)
which is completely free of modular anomalies, and encodes the ‘depth zero’ part of the quasimodular generating series of invariants.

Our second main result is to compute D4-D2-D0 indices (or rank 0 DT invariants) supported on divisors
D=\pi^{*}(\check{D})
pulled back from the base (i.e. D4-branes wrapped both along a basis divisor
\check{D}\subset B
and the generic genus-one fiber) by applying the same monodromy
U
to vertical D2-D0 branes. This generalizes the work
[
26
]
to arbitrary genus one fibrations without fibral divisors. In particular, we show that the holomorphic anomaly equations satisfied by generating series of genus 0 GW invariants at fixed base degree agree with the modular anomaly equations satisfied by rank 0 DT invariants, even though multicover effects are different on both sides.

The rest of this work is organized as follows. In §
2
we set up notations and review basic facts and conjectures about various enumerative invariants of genus one fibered CY threefolds. In §
3
, we focus on base degree zero Gromov-Witten invariants, which are entirely determined by the Euler numbers of the
threefold
X
and the base
B
and by the multiplicities
N_{k}
of fibral curves that intersect the
N
-section
k
times. We express the resulting generating series as linear combinations of Eisenstein series of
\Gamma_{1}(N)
, and (in the genus 0 and genus 1 case) holomorphic Eichler integrals thereof.
These results rely on Proposition
1
as well as the Lemmas
4
,
5
and Theorem
1
, the proofs of which are relegated to Appendix
A
.
In §
4.1
, after reviewing the wave function property of the topological string partition function, we compute the
monodromy matrix implementing the Fourier-Mukai transformation with respect to the ideal sheaf of the relative diagonal, and spell out the resulting transformation properties of the
generating series of Gromov-Witten invariants at fixed base degree. For vanishing base degree, we recover the anomalous transformation properties following from the Eichler integral representations obtained in §
3
. We introduce a new ‘modular polarization’ where modular anomalies are absent, and use it to establish the Jacobi properties of the normalized topological string partition function at fixed base degree. In §
5
, we study the implications of the invariance under the relative conifold monodromy on Donaldson-Thomas invariants (assuming the absence of wall-crossing). In this way we obtain (heuristic) derivations of the elliptic property of the generating series of PT invariants, of the periodicity of base degree zero GV invariants, and of the S-duality property of generating series of MSW invariants counting D4-D2-D0 branes wrapped on the genus fiber (times a fixed divisor on the base). In §
6
we summarize and discuss a few open questions. The remainder of this long paper consists of a suite of appendices collecting background material, technical computations and lots of examples.
In §
A
, we discuss Eisenstein series for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
and prove new results for their transformation under Fricke involutions, expressions in terms of polylogarithms and their holomorphic Eichler integrals.
Further details on the evaluation of the action of the relative conifold monodromy on the Chern classes of branes are provided in §
B
.
In §
C
, we discuss the construction of generic genus one fibrations over generalized del Pezzo surfaces with
N
-sections for
N\leq 5
and provide general expressions for their topological invariants (summarized in Table
3
).
A large set of examples of generic genus one fibered CY threefolds over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with
h^{1,1}=2
is collected/constructed in §
D
, while some examples over other del Pezzo surfaces are discussed in §
E
.
3
3

3

The topological invariants of 72 genus one fibrations over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
are summarized in Tables
5
,
8
,
11
and
13
, and are collected in a
Mathematica
worksheet available with the source code on arXiv.

In both cases we also provide tables of modular generating series of GW invariants at base degree 1 and 2.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we first set up notations and review basic properties of enumerative invariants and topological string amplitudes
on a general smooth projective CY threefold
X
. We then review some of the special properties that have been conjectured to arise in the genus one fibered case.

2.1
Prepotential, topological free energies and Gromov-Witten invariants

Let us denote by
\{C^{a}\}
,
a=1\dots b_{2}(X)=h^{1,1}(X)
a basis of effective curves
in
H_{2}(X,\mathds{Z})
,
\{H_{a}\}
the dual basis in
H_{4}(X,\mathds{Z})

such that
H_{a}\cap C^{b}=\delta_{a}^{b}
, by
\kappa_{abc}=H_{a}\cap H_{b}\cap H_{c}
the triple intersection numbers, and by
c_{2,a}=\int_{H_{a}}c_{2}(TX)
the intersections with the
second Chern class.

At two-derivative order, the low energy effective action describing type IIA string compactified on
X
in the vector multiplet sector is determined by the tree-level prepotential
F^{(0)}(X^{\Lambda})=(X^{0})^{2}\mathcal{F}(t^{a})
, a holomorphic homogeneous function of degree 2 in local coordinates
X^{\Lambda},\Lambda=0,1,\dots,b_{2}(X)
where
t^{a}=X^{a}/X^{0}
,
a=1\dots b_{2}(X)
are the complexified Kähler parameters, such that the complexified Kähler form reads
\omega=t^{a}H_{a}

with
\,{\rm Im}\,t^{a}>0
(here and below, we use the same symbol for an element in
H_{p}(X,\mathds{Z})
and
its Poincaré dual in
H^{6-p}(X,\mathds{Z})
).
4
4

4

If the Kähler cone is non-simplicial, then
H_{a}
have to be choosen such that they generate
H^{2}(X,\mathds{Z})
and
\,{\rm Im}\,t^{a}>0
is a simplicial sub-cone of the Kähler cone.

In the large volume limit
t^{a}\to\mathrm{i}\infty
, one has
5
5

5

Here we use a non-integral basis,
referred to as
primed
basis in
[
46
]
, that differs slightly from the integral basis typically used in the topological string literature, but which allows to get rid of quadratic and linear terms in
t^{a}
.

\mathcal{F}^{(0)}(t^{a})=\frac{1}{6}(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}\kappa_{abc}t^{a}t^{b}t^{c}-\frac{1}{2}\zeta(3)\chi_{X}+\sum_{\beta_{a}>0}{\rm GW}^{(0)}_{\beta_{a}}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}}

(2.1)

where
{\rm GW}^{(0)}_{\beta_{a}}
are the genus 0 Gromov-Witten invariants counting rational curves in
X
of homology class
\beta_{a}C^{a}
(with
\beta_{a}>0
meaning that

\beta_{a}\geq 0
for all
a=1\dots b_{2}(X)
, not all vanishing at once). The homogeneous prepotential
F^{(0)}
can be understood as the generating function of a Lagrangian subspace
F_{\Lambda}=\partial_{X^{\Lambda}}F^{(0)}
inside the complex vector space
\mathds{C}^{2b_{2}(X)+2}\sim H^{\rm even}(X,\mathds{C})
with coordinates
V=(F_{\Lambda},X^{\Lambda})^{t}
equipped with the symplectic form
\omega=\mathrm{d}X^{\Lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d}F_{\Lambda}
. As a result, under a monodromy

V=\begin{pmatrix}F_{\Lambda}\\
X^{\Lambda}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto V^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F_{\Lambda}\\
X^{\Lambda}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(2.2)

where
A,B,C,D
are square matrices of size
b_{2}(X)
such that

A^{T}D-C^{T}B=1,\quad A^{T}C=C^{T}A,\quad BD^{T}=D^{T}B\,,

(2.3)

the prepotential transforms as
[
55
]

F(X^{\Lambda})\mapsto F^{\prime}(X^{\prime\Lambda})=F(X^{\Lambda})-S(X^{\Lambda},X^{\prime\Lambda})

(2.4)

where, assuming that the matrix
C
is invertible,

S(X^{\Lambda},X^{\prime\Lambda})=-\frac{1}{2}X^{\Lambda}(C^{-1}D)_{\Lambda\Sigma}X^{\Sigma}+X^{\Lambda}C^{-1}_{\Lambda\Sigma}X^{\prime\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}X^{\prime\Lambda}(AC^{-1})_{\Lambda\Sigma}X^{\prime\Sigma}

(2.5)

satisfies
\partial_{X^{\Lambda}}S=F_{\Lambda},\partial_{X^{\prime\Lambda}}S=-F^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
, such that the r.h.s. of (
2.4
) is extremized with respect to
X^{\Lambda}
.

At higher order in the derivative expansion, special protected couplings are governed by the topological free energies
\mathcal{F}^{(g)}(t^{a},\bar{t}^{a})
. These observables are not holomorphic, but satisfy holomorphic anomaly equations which will be reviewed in §
4.1
.
The holomorphic free energies
F^{(g)}:=(X^{0})^{2-2g}\mathcal{F}^{(g)}(t^{a})

are defined as the holomorphic limit
\bar{t}^{a}\to-\mathrm{i}\infty
keeping
t^{a}
fixed.
Near the large volume point, the
\mathcal{F}^{(g)}
’s are determined by the Gromov-Witten invariants
{\rm GW}^{(g)}_{\beta_{a}}
counting stable maps from a curve of genus
g
into
X
with image in the class
\beta_{a}C^{a}
, up to a classical, linear term at genus 1, and a constant map contribution at genus
g\geq 2
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}(t^{a})=&\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{24}c_{2,a}t^{a}+\sum\limits_{\beta_{a}>0}^{\infty}{\rm GW}^{(1)}_{\beta_{a}}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}}\,,\\
\mathcal{F}^{(g\geq 2)}(t^{a})=&\frac{(-1)^{g-1}B_{2g}B_{2g-2}}{(2g)(2g-2)(2g-2)!}\chi_{X}+\sum\limits_{\beta_{a}>0}^{\infty}{\rm GW}^{(g)}_{\beta_{a}}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}}\,.\end{split}

(2.6)

The topological string partition function on
X
is defined as a formal asymptotic series

Z_{\rm top}(t^{a},\lambda):=\lambda^{\frac{\chi_{X}}{24}-1}e^{\sum_{g\geq 0}\lambda^{2g-2}\mathcal{F}^{(g)}(t^{a})}\,,

(2.7)

where
\lambda
is the topological string coupling. We note that the
\chi_{X}
-dependent terms are all captured by a power of the MacMahon function
6
6

6

The asymptotic expansion of the (logarithm of the) MacMahon function was first worked out in
[
56
, (E.32)]
, and later rederived in a much simpler way in

[
57
, (4.37)]
. The two expressions agree after correcting

\zeta^{\prime}(1)
into
\zeta^{\prime}(-1)
in
[
57
, (4.37)]
, and using the identity

\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\zeta^{\prime}(2)+\frac{1}{12}\gamma_{E}=-\zeta^{\prime}(-1)+\frac{1}{12}
. There remains a discrepancy by the additive constant
\frac{1}{12}
, which was apparently missed in
[
56
]
.

\begin{split}M(\lambda):=&\prod_{k\geq 1}(1-e^{\mathrm{i}k\lambda})^{-k}\\
=&\exp\left[-\frac{\zeta(3)}{\lambda^{2}}+\frac{1}{12}\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)+\zeta^{\prime}(-1)+\sum_{g\geq 2}(-1)^{g-1}\tfrac{B_{2g}B_{2g-2}\lambda^{2g-2}}{(2g)(2g-2)(2g-2)!}\right]\,.\end{split}

(2.8)

In §
4.1
we shall explain that

Z_{\rm top}
transforms as a wave-function under monodromies in Kähler moduli space,
hence specifying the transformation properties of the respective holomorphic free energies
F^{(g)}
.

GW invariants
are in general rational numbers, but can be expressed in terms of the integer-valued
Gopakumar-Vafa (GV) invariants
{\rm GV}^{(g)}_{\beta_{a}}
by the multi-cover formula
[
58
,
59
,
60
]

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{g=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{\beta_{a}>0}{\rm GW}^{(g)}_{\beta_{a}}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}}\lambda^{2g-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{g=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{\beta_{a}>0}\frac{{\rm GV}^{(g)}_{\beta_{a}}}{k}\left[2\sin\left(\frac{k\lambda}{2}\right)\right]^{2g-2}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k\beta_{a}t^{a}}\,.

(2.9)

At genus 0, this reduces to the standard multicover formula for rational curves

{\rm GW}^{(0)}_{\beta_{a}}=\sum_{d|\beta_{a}}\frac{1}{d^{3}}{\rm GV}^{(0)}_{\beta_{a}/d}
.
While GW invariants are supported on the Mori cone
\beta_{a}>0
, GV invariants are typically supported on a smaller cone, sometimes called the
‘infinity cone’
[
61
]
, up to a few
so-called nilpotent rays, which by definition support a finite number of non-vanishing genus 0 GV invariants.
The infinity cone is given by the intersection of the Mori cones of all
CY threefolds birationally equivalent to
X
, obtained by flopping the curves associated to the nilpotent rays, see
[
61
]
for a more precise statement.
Moreover, for fixed class
\beta_{a}
,
{\rm GV}^{(g)}_{\beta_{a}}
vanishes when
g>g_{\rm max}(\beta_{a})
is large enough
[
62
]
.

2.2
BPS states and Donaldson-Thomas invariants

BPS states correspond to stable objects
E
in the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}{\rm Coh}(X)
. This category is graded by the Chern character
\ch(E)
, related to the electromagnetic charge
\gamma=(p^{0},p^{a};q_{a},q_{0})
(in the ‘primed basis’, see footnote
5
)

p^{0}=\ch_{0},\quad p^{a}=\int_{C^{a}}\ch_{1},\quad q_{a}=-\int_{H_{a}}(\ch_{2}+\tfrac{c_{2}(TX)}{24}\ch_{0}),\quad q_{0}=\int_{X}(\ch_{3}+\tfrac{c_{2}(TX)}{24}\ch_{1}),

(2.10)

such that the following quantization conditions are satisfied:

p^{0},p^{a}\in\mathds{Z},\quad q_{a}\in\mathds{Z}-\frac{c_{2,a}}{24}p^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{abc}p^{b}p^{c},\quad q_{0}\in\mathds{Z}-\frac{c_{2,a}}{24}p^{a}

(2.11)

We denote by
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{Q}^{2b_{2}(X)+2}
the lattice specified by these quantization conditions, and by
\gamma=(p^{0},p^{a};q_{a},q_{0})
a generic lattice vector.

\Gamma
is equipped with an antisymmetric integer pairing, known as the Dirac-Schwinger-Zwanziger product, or Euler form,

\langle\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\rangle=q_{0}p^{\prime 0}+q_{a}p^{\prime a}-q^{\prime}_{a}p^{a}-q^{\prime}_{0}p^{0}

(2.12)

Donaldson-Thomas invariants depend on a choice of stability condition
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
, where

Z
is a central charge function and
\mathcal{A}
an Abelian subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
, determined locally by
Z
, satisfying various axioms. Physical stability conditions are those where the central charge
is determined by the tree-level prepotential,

Z_{t}(\gamma)=q_{0}+q_{a}t^{a}-p^{a}\partial_{t^{a}}\mathcal{F}^{(0)}-p^{0}(2\mathcal{F}^{(0)}-t^{a}\partial_{t^{a}}\mathcal{F}^{(0)})

(2.13)

such that
|Z_{t}(\gamma)|
measures the mass of a BPS state with electromagnetic charge
\gamma
.
In the large volume limit
t^{a}\to\mathrm{i}\infty
,
Z_{t}
is determined by the Chern character of the object
E
,

Z_{t}(E)\sim\int_{X}e^{-t^{a}H_{a}}\ch(E)\left(1+\frac{c_{2}(TX)}{24}\right)

(2.14)

up to terms proportional to
\zeta(3)\chi_{X}
,
arising from
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{\prime})^{3}
corrections in string theory,
and up to exponentially suppressed terms as
t^{a}\to\mathrm{i}\infty
,
corresponding to worldsheet instanton corrections. We choose
an Abelian subcategory
\mathcal{A}_{t}
compatible
7
7

7

Such an Abelian subcategory, or more precisely heart of
t
-structure, exists provided
X
satisfies the Bayer-Macrì-Toda inequality, which remains conjectural for the CY threefolds of interest in this paper. See
[
12
]
for references and introduction to stability conditions on CY threefolds aimed at physicists.
with
Z_{t}
, and denote by
\Omega_{t}(\gamma)
the Donaldson-Thomas invariant
counting semi-stable objects of charge
\gamma
for the stability condition
\sigma_{t}=(Z_{t},\mathcal{A}_{t})
.
When
\sigma_{t}
is generic, this is an integer number, corresponding physically to the
index counting (with signs) BPS states of charge
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
We further define the rational DT invariant
\bar{\Omega}_{t}(\gamma)=\sum_{d|\gamma}d^{-2}\Omega_{t}(\gamma/d)
, which has simpler behavior under wall-crossing
[
63
]
. Importantly,
\Omega_{t}(\gamma)
and its rational counterpart are invariant under complex structure deformations, and under monodromies in Kähler moduli space, in the sense that

\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)=\Omega_{g\cdot\sigma}(\gamma\cdot g)

(2.15)

where
g\cdot\sigma
is the action of the auto-equivalence
g\in{\rm Aut}(\mathcal{C})
implementing the monodromy (acting conventionally from the left on
\sigma
), while
\gamma\cdot g
is its action on the charge row vector (acting conventionally from the right).
In particular,
\Omega_{t}(\gamma)
is invariant under the large volume monodromy

\displaystyle p^{0}\mapsto p^{0},\quad p^{a}\mapsto p^{a}+p^{0}\epsilon^{a},\quad q_{a}\mapsto q_{a}-\kappa_{abc}p^{b}\epsilon^{c}-\frac{1}{2}p^{0}\kappa_{abc}\epsilon^{b}\epsilon^{c},\quad

\displaystyle q_{0}\mapsto q_{0}-q_{a}\epsilon^{a}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{abc}p^{a}\epsilon^{b}\epsilon^{c}+\frac{1}{6}p^{0}\kappa_{abc}\epsilon^{b}\epsilon^{c}\epsilon^{c}

(2.16)

provided the Kähler moduli (and the corresponding stability condition) are shifted as

t^{a}\mapsto t^{a}+\epsilon^{a}
(for any
\epsilon^{a}\in\mathds{Z}
).
DT invariants may however jump on certain walls of marginal stability where the phase of
Z_{t}(E)
aligns with the phase of one of the subobjects of
E
. In particular, the r.h.s. of (
2.15
) need
not be equal to
\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma\cdot h)
.

For
p^{0}=p^{a}=0
, the invariant
\Omega_{t^{a}\to\mathrm{i}\infty}(0,0;\beta_{a}=q_{a},q_{0})
counting semi-stable sheaves supported on the effective curve
C=\beta_{a}C^{a}
is expected to be independent of
q_{0}
, and to coincide with the genus 0 Gopakumar-Vafa invariant
{\rm GV}^{(0)}_{\beta_{a}}

[
64
]
.
Note however that its rational counterpart
\bar{\Omega}_{t^{a}\to\mathrm{i}\infty}(0,0;q_{a},q_{0})
is
not
independent of the D0-brane charge (or Euler number of the sheaf)
q_{0}
, and differs from the genus 0 GW invariant due to different multi-cover effects.

For
p^{0}=-1,p^{a}=0
,
the invariant
\Omega_{t\to e^{\mathrm{i}\pi/3}\infty}(-1,0;\beta_{a}+\frac{c_{2,a}}{24},-m)
in a suitable large volume, large B-field limit coincides with the invariant
\operatorname{PT}(\beta_{a},m)
counting stable pairs

E:\mathcal{O}_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\to}}F
where
F
is a pure one-dimensional sheaf with

\ch_{2}(F)=\beta_{a}C^{a}
and
\chi(F)=m
, and
s
is a section of
F
with zero-dimensional kernel

[
65
]
. PT invariants vanish for
m\ll 0
, and
are related to GV invariants via
[
5
,
66
]

\begin{split}\sum_{\beta_{a}>0,m}\operatorname{PT}(\beta_{a},m)\,e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}}\mbox{q}^{m}=&\prod_{\beta_{a}>0}\prod_{k>0}\left(1-(-\mbox{q})^{k}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}}\right)^{k{\rm GV}^{(0)}_{\beta_{a}}}\\
\times\prod_{\beta_{a}>0}\prod_{g=1}^{g_{\rm max}(\beta)}&\prod_{\ell=0}^{2g-2}\left(1-(-\mbox{q})^{g-\ell-1}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}}\right)^{(-1)^{g+\ell}{\scriptsize{\begin{pmatrix}2g-2\\
\ell\end{pmatrix}}}{\rm GV}^{(g)}_{\beta_{a}}}\,.\end{split}

(2.17)

Upon identifying
\mbox{q}=-e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}
,
the right-hand side is recognized as the topological
string partition function, up to a power of the MacMahon function and a polynomial term coming from classical contributions at genus zero and one,

Z_{\rm top}(t^{a},\lambda)=M(\lambda)^{\frac{\chi_{X}}{2}}\,e^{F_{\rm pol}(t^{a},\lambda)}\,\sum_{\beta_{a},m}\operatorname{PT}(\beta_{a},m)\,e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\beta_{a}t^{a}+\mathrm{i}m\lambda}\,.

(2.18)

2.3
Modularity of D4-D2-D0 indices

In general, the generating series of rational D4-D2-D0 indices with fixed D4-brane charge
p^{a}
associated to an ample divisor class
\mathcal{D}_{p}=p^{a}H_{a}

and D2-brane charge
\mu_{a}
, evaluated in the so-called large volume attractor chamber,

h_{p;\mu}(\tau)=\sum_{\hat{q}_{0}\leq\frac{\chi(\mathcal{D}_{p})}{24}}\overline{\Omega}_{p;\mu}(\hat{q}_{0})\,e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\tau\hat{q}_{0}}

(2.19)

transforms as a vector valued mock modular form of weight
-1-\frac{b_{2}(X)}{2}
under
SL(2,\mathds{Z})

[
67
,
68
,
15
,
14
,
69
]
(see

[
70
,
71
]
for early work on this topic, and
[
72
]
for a recent review).
Here
\hat{q}_{0}
is the
invariant D0-brane charge

\hat{q}_{0}=q_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\kappa^{ab}q_{a}q_{b}

(2.20)

where
\kappa^{ab}
is the inverse
8
8

8

When
\mathcal{D}_{p}
is ample,
the matrix
\kappa_{ab}
has signature
(1,b_{2}(X)-1)
and is invertible by the Hodge index theorem.

matrix to
\kappa_{ab}:=\kappa_{abc}p^{c}
,
\mu_{a}
labels the coset in
\Lambda^{*}/\Lambda
(of cardinality
|\,{\rm det}\,(\kappa_{ab})|
), with
\Lambda=H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, such that

q_{a}=\mu_{a}+\kappa_{ab}\epsilon^{b}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{abc}p^{b}p^{c}

(2.21)

and
\chi(\mathcal{D}_{p})=\kappa_{abc}p^{a}p^{b}p^{c}+c_{2,a}p^{a}
is the Euler number of a divisor
\mathcal{D}_{p}
with class
p
.
More precisely, there exists a canonical completion

\widehat{h}_{p;\mu}(\tau):=h_{p;\mu}(\tau)+\sum_{n\geq 2}\sum_{\check{\gamma}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\check{\gamma}_{i}}R_{n}\left(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\},\tau_{2}\right)\,e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau\,Q_{n}(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{p_{i},\mu_{i}}(\tau)

(2.22)

where
\check{\gamma}=(p^{a},q_{a})
denotes the vector of D4 and D2 charges,
Q_{n}
is a quadratic form of signature
((n-1)(b_{2}(X)-1),n-1)
,

Q_{n}(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\})=\kappa^{ab}q_{a}q_{b}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}^{ab}q_{i,a}q_{i,,b}

(2.23)

and
R_{n}\left(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\},\tau_{2}\right)
a sum of products of generalized error functions and derivatives thereof
[
73
,
74
,
75
]
, such that

\widehat{h}_{p;\mu}

transforms as a vector valued modular form
with weight
-1-\frac{b_{2}(X)}{2}
in the Weil representation of
\Lambda^{*}/\Lambda
. In particular, under the
S:\tau\mapsto-1/\tau
and
T:\tau\mapsto\tau+1
transformations, it transforms by the matrices

[
14
, Eq.(2.10)]
(see also
[
76
,
77
,
78
,
79
]
)

\begin{split}M_{\mu\nu}(T)=&\,e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\left(\mu+\frac{p}{2}\right)^{2}+\tfrac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\,c_{2,a}p^{a}}\,\delta_{\mu\nu},\\
M_{\mu\nu}(S)=&\,\frac{(-1)^{\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}_{p}})}}{\sqrt{|\Lambda^{*}/\Lambda|}}\,e^{(b_{2}(X)-2)\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\,e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mu\cdot\nu}\,,\end{split}

(2.24)

where
\mu\cdot\nu=\kappa^{ab}\mu_{a}\nu_{b}
,

\delta_{\mu\nu}
is the Kronecker delta on the discriminant group
\Lambda^{*}/\Lambda
, and

\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}_{p}})=\frac{1}{2}(b_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{D}_{p})+1)
is the arithmetic genus given by

\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}_{p}})=\frac{1}{6}\,\kappa_{abc}p^{a}p^{b}p^{c}+\frac{1}{12}\,c_{2,a}p^{a}\,.

(2.25)

While the modular completion (
2.22
) is not holomorphic, due to the kernels

R_{n}\left(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\},\tau_{2}\right)
, its
\bar{\tau}
-derivative is simply determined by the
modular completions of the generating series of the constituents, as

\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{h}_{p;\mu}(\tau)=\sum_{n\geq 2}\sum_{\check{\gamma}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\check{\gamma}_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{n}\left(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\},\tau_{2}\right)\,e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau\,Q_{n}(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{h}_{p_{i},\mu_{i}}(\tau)

(2.26)

where the kernels
\mathcal{J}_{n}\left(\{\check{\gamma}_{i}\},\tau_{2}\right)
are again sums of products
of generalized error functions, this time leading to a modular theta series, consistent with the fact
that
\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
raises the modular weight by 2 units.

Up to now, we assumed that the quadratic form
\kappa_{ab}=\kappa_{abc}p^{c}
was invertible, and that the vector
p^{a}
had positive norm,
\kappa_{ab}p^{a}p^{b}>0
. Both conditions are automatically satisfied when the divisor class
p^{a}H_{a}
is ample (see footnote
8
), however they fail for the D4-D2-D0 indices related to D2-D0 indices by a relative conifold monodromy, as we shall see in §
5
. Generalizations of the modularity constraints were conjectured in
[
80
]
in the case where
\kappa_{ab}
is degenerate, and in
[
72
]
in the case where the vector
p^{a}
is isotropic,
\kappa_{ab}p^{a}p^{b}=0
.
We now briefly summarize the resulting prescriptions, while pointing out that it is an open problem to characterize the modular properties of generating series for arbitrary, non-ample effective divisor classes.

Starting with the case where
\kappa_{ab}
is degenerate, let
\{\lambda_{s}^{a}\}
be a set of null vectors, i.e.
\kappa_{ab}\lambda_{s}^{a}=0
, and
\Lambda_{p}\subset\Lambda
the sublattice orthogonal to these vectors,

\Lambda_{p}=\{q_{a}\in\mathds{Z}^{b_{2}(X)}+\frac{1}{2}\,\kappa_{ab}p^{b}\ :\ \lambda_{s}^{a}q_{a}=0\}.

(2.27)

We now introduce the pseudo-inverse quadratic form
9
9

9

This is also known as the Moore-Penrose inverse.

\kappa^{ab}
defined by the conditions
i)
\mbox{rank}(\kappa^{ab})=\mbox{rank}(\kappa_{ab})
, ii)
\kappa^{ac}\kappa_{cb}=\delta^{a}_{b}-\sum_{s,t}e^{st}\lambda_{s}^{a}\lambda_{t}^{b}

where
e^{st}
is the inverse of
e_{st}=\sum_{a}\lambda_{s}^{a}\lambda_{t}^{a}
.
Then the claim of
[
80
]
is that, upon replacing
\Lambda
by
\Lambda_{p}
and
b_{2}(X)
by
\mbox{rank}(\Lambda_{p})
,
all results previously stated for ample divisors continue to apply. In particular, the modular weight of the generating function is now
-1-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{rank}(\Lambda_{p})
. In the special case where
p^{a}
itself is a null vector (i.e.
\kappa_{ab}p^{b}=0
), as happens in the case of vertical D4-D2-D0 invariants in K3-fibered CY threefolds
[
22
]
, the modular anomaly turns out to be absent, so that

\widehat{h}_{p;\mu}
coincides with
h_{p;\mu}
for any
p
, reducible or not.

Next we turn to the case where
p^{a}
is an isotropic vector,
\kappa_{ab}p^{a}p^{b}=0
with
\kappa_{ab}
being non-degenerate. In this case, the modular anomaly is still present but it significantly simplifies. For simplicity, we assume that
p^{a}
lies along the boundary of the effective cone, such that the only allowed splittings involve collinear charges,
p_{i}^{a}=r_{i}p_{0}^{a}
where

p_{0}^{a}
is a primitive vector with
p_{0}^{3}:=\kappa_{abc}p_{0}^{a}p_{0}^{b}p_{0}^{c}=0
.
In this
collinear case
, only binary splittings occur on the r.h.s. of (
2.26
)
[
14
, 5.2]
. The isotropic case
p_{0}^{3}=0
can be obtained as a limit
\epsilon\to 0

of a positive vector
p_{\epsilon}=p_{0}+\epsilon v_{1}
, where
v_{1}
is any lattice vector
with positive inner product
\xi:=p_{0}\cdot v_{1}>0
. While
the coefficient
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is in general proportional to
\sqrt{p_{\epsilon}^{3}}
, which vanishes in the limit

\epsilon\to 0
, it also involves a theta series which diverges in the same limit. In
[
72
, §D]
, it is shown that the result is finite in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, and given by

\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{h}_{rp_{0},\mu}=\frac{1}{16\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{r=r_{1}+r_{2}}\mathcal{J}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\,\widehat{h}_{r_{1}p_{0},\mu}\,\widehat{h}_{r_{2}p_{0},\mu}

(2.28)

where

\mathcal{J}_{r_{1},r_{2}}=r_{0}\sum_{\mu_{1}\in\Lambda_{1}^{*}/\Lambda_{1},\mu_{2}\in\Lambda_{2}^{*}/\Lambda_{2}}\delta_{\mu-\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\in r_{0}\Lambda_{0}}\sum_{A=0}^{n_{g}-1}\delta^{(\xi r_{12})}_{p_{0}\cdot(\mu_{12}^{\parallel}+r_{12}g_{A}^{\parallel})}\vartheta^{\perp}_{\mu_{12}^{\perp}+r_{12}g_{A}^{\perp}}\,.

(2.29)

Here
r_{0}=\gcd(r_{1},r_{2})
,
r_{12}=\frac{rr_{1}r_{2}}{r_{0}^{2}}
;

\Lambda_{0}
is the lattice
\Lambda
equipped with the quadratic form
\kappa_{0,ab}=\kappa_{abc}p_{0}^{c}
,
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
are the same lattices with quadratic form
rescaled by
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, respectively; the lattice
\Lambda_{0}^{\parallel}
is the two-dimensional
sublattice of
\Lambda_{0}
spanned by
(p_{0},v_{1})
, and
\Lambda_{0}^{\perp}
is its orthogonal complement; the glue vectors
(g_{A}^{\parallel},g_{A}^{\perp}),A=1,\dots n_{g}

generate the quotient
\Lambda_{0}/(\Lambda_{0}^{\parallel}\oplus\Lambda_{0}^{\perp})
,
and
\delta^{(N)}_{m}
is the Kronecker delta on
\mathds{Z}_{N}
, equal to 1 if
m=0\mod N
and 0 otherwise.
Finally, the factor
\mu_{12}
is defined as

r_{0}\,\mu_{12,a}=r_{2}\mu_{1,a}-r_{1}\mu_{2,a}+r_{1}r_{2}\kappa_{0,ab}\left(\rho_{1}^{b}-\rho_{2}^{b}+\frac{1}{2}(r_{1}-r_{2})p_{0}^{b}\right)\,,

(2.30)

where
\rho_{1}^{a},\rho_{2}^{a}
are any integer solutions of

\mu_{a}-\mu_{1,a}-\mu_{2,a}+r_{1}r_{2}\kappa_{0,ab}p_{0}^{b}=\kappa_{0,ab}\left(r_{1}\rho_{1}^{b}+r_{2}\rho_{2}^{b}\right)

(2.31)

(which exist by virtue of the condition
\mu-\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\in r_{0}\Lambda_{0}
).
While these various definitions depend on the choice of vectors
v_{1},\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
,
the final result does not. Of course, it is advisable
to choose
v_{1}
in such a way that the number of glue vectors is minimal. In the case of
interest in §
5
, we shall have to deal at the same time with a
degenerate quadratic form
\kappa_{ab}
and an isotropic vector
p^{a}
, but the lattice

\Lambda_{0}^{\perp}
will be null such that the theta series
\vartheta^{\perp}_{\mu_{12}^{\perp}+r_{12}g_{A}^{\perp}}
will actually be a constant.

2.4
Genus one fibrations and Jacobi forms

We now assume that
X
is a CY threefold with a torus fibration
F\xhookrightarrow{}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}B

over a generalized del Pezzo surface
B
. By this we mean that

B
is either
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{2}
or an iterative blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
k=0,\ldots,8
points that are not necessarily generic but do not lie on any curve of self-intersection
-2
.
For simplicity, we shall focus on sufficiently generic fibrations such that
b_{2}(X)=b_{2}(B)+1
.
10
10

10

This implies in particular that the fibration does not exhibit any fibral divisors, that resolve singularities over one-dimensional components of the discriminant locus, or additional
N
-sections that correspond to a non-zero Mordell-Weil rank of the associated Jacobian fibration.

We also assume that the complex structure is sufficiently generic, such that the discriminant divisor in
B
is reduced and irreducible and has at most isolated nodes and cusps as singularities.
For a more detailed discussion of such geometries, see
[
40
, Section 3]
.

Let us first introduce some terminology.
A divisor
S
is called an
N
-section
if
\pi(S)=B
and there exists a dense open subset
U\subset B
such that
S\big|_{U}
is an
N
-sheeted branched covering of
U
.
This implies that
F\cap S=N
.
We further assume that the covering is irreducible and refer to divisors that are unions of
N_{i}
-sections with
\sum_{i}N_{i}=N
as
pseudo
N
-sections
.
A
1
-section is just a section.

If the fibration has a section it is called
elliptic
.
On the other hand, if it only exhibits an
N
-section – and there are no
N^{\prime}
-sections with
N^{\prime}<N
– we refer to it as a
genus one fibration with an
N
-section
.
We use the term
torus fibration
if we want to remain agnostic about the existence of a section.
Every torus fibration has an
N
-section for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

We choose a basis
\{\check{D}^{\alpha},\alpha=1,\ldots,b_{2}(B)\}
of effective curve classes on
B
, with intersection form
C^{\alpha\beta}=\check{D}^{\alpha}\cap\check{D}^{\beta}
, and denote by

\check{D}_{\alpha}=C_{\alpha\beta}\check{D}^{\beta}\in\Pic(B)
the dual basis such that

\check{D}^{\alpha}\cap\check{D}_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
(here
C_{\alpha\beta}
is the inverse of
C^{\alpha\beta}
; note that
\,{\rm det}\,C_{\alpha\beta}=1
as the lattice
H_{2}(B,\mathds{Z})
is self-dual).
If the Kähler cone of
B
is simplicial, we assume that the divisor classes
\check{D}_{\alpha}
form a basis.
If it is not simplicial, we assume that the
\check{D}_{\alpha}

span a simplicial sub-cone of the Kähler cone and that all integral divisors inside the Kähler cone are linear combinations with integer coefficients of the
\check{D}_{\alpha}
’s.
We denote by
c_{1}(B)=a_{\alpha}\check{D}^{\alpha}=a^{\alpha}\check{D}_{\alpha}
the first Chern class of
B
.

A basis of effective divisors
\{D_{a}\}=\{D_{e}\}\cup\{D_{\alpha},\alpha=1,\ldots,b_{2}(B)\}

in
H_{4}(X,\mathds{Z})
can be assembled by combining the class of the
N
-section
D_{e}
with pullbacks
D_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}(\check{D}_{\alpha})
of effective divisors on the base. By construction, the divisors
D_{\alpha}
have vanishing intersections,

D_{\alpha}\cap D_{\beta}\cap D_{\gamma}=0
,
while their intersection with
D_{e}
is proportional
to their intersection on the base,
D_{e}\cap D_{\alpha}\cap D_{\beta}=NC_{\alpha\beta}
.
We denote the intersection numbers by
\kappa_{abc}:=D_{a}\cap D_{b}\cap D_{c}
and introduce
\kappa,\ell_{\alpha}
such that

\kappa:=\kappa_{eee}\,,\quad\ell_{\alpha}:=\kappa_{ee\alpha}\,,\quad\kappa_{e\alpha\beta}=NC_{\alpha\beta},\quad\kappa_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=0

(2.32)

and denote the integrals of the second Chern class
c_{2}(TX)
on
(D_{e},D_{\alpha})
by

c_{2}:=c_{2,e},\quad c_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{12}c_{2,\alpha}\,.

(2.33)

In fact, the coefficients
c_{\alpha}
appear to be always equal to the coefficients
a_{\alpha}
of the anti-canonical class on the base, but since we are not aware of a proof in general, we use a different notation.
11
11

11

For elliptic fibrations with a section this is proven for example in
[
81
, Appendix D]
and for certain genus one fibrations with
N
-sections a proof can be found in
[
33
, Appendix B]
. A sketch of a more general proof is discussed in
[
40
, Section 3.3]
and a physical derivation can be found in
[
40
, Section 4.5]
by comparing the Chern-Simons terms of five-dimensional F- and M-theory compactifications.

Importantly, while the divisors
D_{\alpha}
are always nef, and
D_{e}
is always effective,
D_{e}
is not necessarily nef.
One can however always shift it by a linear combination
D_{e}\mapsto D_{e}+\eta^{\alpha}D_{\alpha}
, such that the resulting divisor is nef and such that
\{D_{e},D_{\alpha}\}
generates the nef cone (defined as the closure of the Kähler/ample cone). Under this shift, the intersection coefficients (
2.32
) transform as

\ell_{\alpha}\mapsto\ell_{\alpha}+2NC_{\alpha\beta}\eta^{\beta},\quad\kappa\mapsto\kappa+3\ell_{\alpha}\eta^{\alpha}+3NC_{\alpha\beta}\eta^{\alpha}\eta^{\beta}\,,

(2.34)

leaving the following combinations invariant,

\widehat{\kappa}:=\kappa-\frac{3}{4N}\ell_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}\ell_{\beta},\quad\widehat{c}_{2}:=c_{2}-\frac{6}{N}c^{\alpha}\ell_{\alpha}\,.

(2.35)

We will refer to curves that are supported on a single fiber of the fibration as
fibral curves
.
The fibers of the fibration are irreducible except for
I_{2}
-fibers over isolated points of the base.
The
I_{2}
-fibers consist of two rational curves that intersect transversely in two points.
We denote by
N_{k}
,
k=1,\ldots,N-1
the number of fibral curves that intersect the
N
-section
k
times.
By comparing the Chern-Simons terms of the corresponding F- and M-theory vacua, it was conjectured in
[
40
, Conj. 3]
that the invariant combinations (
2.35
)
are determined in terms of these numbers by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\kappa}&=&\frac{12-\chi_{B}}{4}N^{3}-\frac{1}{8N}\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}k^{2}(N-k)^{2}N_{k}\,,\\
\widehat{c}_{2}&=&4(15-\chi_{B})N-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}k(N-k)N_{k}\,.\end{split}

(2.36)

Moreover, the
N_{k}
’s along with the Euler numbers
\chi_{X}
and
\chi_{B}
determine all non-trivial Gopakumar-Vafa invariants at base degree
zero
[
82
]
12
12

12

This statement generalizes
[
83
, Thm 6.9]
to the case of torus fibrations. We do not know of a rigorous mathematical proof in general, but we shall give a heuristic argument of the mod
N
periodicity of
{\rm GV}^{(0)}_{n,0}
using
invariance under the relative conifold monodromy in §
4.3
.
,

{\rm GV}^{(0)}_{mN+k,0_{\alpha}}=N_{k},\quad{\rm GV}^{(0)}_{(m+1)N,0_{\alpha}}=-\chi_{X},\quad{\rm GV}^{(1)}_{m+1,0_{\alpha}}=\chi_{B}

(2.37)

for all
m\in\mathbb{N},\quad k=1,\ldots,N-1
. Moreover, the following sum rule

\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\rm GV}^{(0)}_{k,0_{\alpha}}=60(12-\chi_{B})\,,

(2.38)

is required by the cancellation of gravitational anomalies in F-theory
[
84
]
. For
N=1
, this sum rule reduces to
\chi_{X}=-60(12-\chi_{B})
.
Since the base is a rational surface, we also have
\chi_{B}=12-a_{\alpha}a^{\alpha}
.

Now, we denote by
\{C^{a}\}=\{\mathcal{E}\}\cup\{C^{\alpha},\alpha=1,\dots b_{2}(B)\}
the basis of curve classes in
H_{2}(X,\mathds{Z})
dual to
\{D_{a}\}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cap D_{e}=1\,,\quad\mathcal{E}\cap D_{\alpha}=C^{\alpha}\cap D_{e}=0\,,\quad C^{\alpha}\cap D_{\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\gamma}\,.

(2.39)

The curve class
C^{e}:=\mathcal{E}
is related to the class of the generic fiber
F
via
F=N\mathcal{E}
such that
F\cap D_{e}=N
, consistent with the fact that
D_{e}
is the class of the
N
-section. The curve classes
C^{\alpha}
are linear combinations of
F
and of the intersections
D_{e}\cap D_{\alpha}
,

C^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{N}C^{\alpha\beta}\left(D_{e}\cap D_{\beta}-\frac{\ell_{\beta}}{N}F\right)\,.

(2.40)

Note that
C^{\alpha}
is an integral class, despite the factors of
1/N
appearing in this expression.
For
N=1
, i.e. in the presence of a section
\sigma
, the intersection
D_{e}D_{\alpha}
is identical with the embedding
\sigma(\check{D}_{\alpha})
of the curve on
B
, but differs from
C_{\alpha\beta}C^{\beta}
by a multiple of the fiber class.

As in §
2.1
, the Kähler form
\omega
can be expanded as a linear combination of the Poincaré dual of the basis of divisors
\{D_{e},D_{\alpha}\}
,

\omega=TD_{e}+S^{\alpha}D_{\alpha}\,.

(2.41)

In order to state the modular properties of the generating series of Gromov-Witten invariants, it is useful to expand
\omega
in a different basis
\{\tilde{D}_{e}:=D_{e}+\frac{1}{2N}D,D_{\alpha}\}

where
D
is the pull-back of the so called height pairing
[
30
,
33
]
,

\displaystyle D:=-\pi^{*}\pi_{*}(D_{e}D_{e})=-\ell_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}D_{\beta}\,,

(2.42)

and parametrize instead the Kähler form as

\omega=T\tilde{D}_{e}+\widehat{S}^{\alpha}D_{\alpha}\ ,\quad\widehat{S}^{\alpha}:=S^{\alpha}+\frac{T}{2N}C^{\alpha\beta}\ell_{\beta}\,.

(2.43)

Although
\tilde{D}_{e}
is in general not an integral class, it is worth noting that it is invariant
under shifts
D_{e}\mapsto D_{e}-\eta^{\alpha}D_{\alpha}
accompanied by the corresponding change
(
2.34
) in the intersection numbers. In fact, the invariants
\widehat{\kappa}
and
\widehat{c}_{2}

respectively are the self-intersection
\tilde{D}_{e}^{3}
and second Chern class
\tilde{D}_{e}\cap c_{2}(TX)
for the non-integral class
\tilde{D}_{e}
, which satisfies
\tilde{D}_{e}\cap\tilde{D}_{e}\cap D_{\alpha}=0
.

In terms of the parametrization (
2.43
), one may define generating series of Gromov-Witten invariants at fixed genus
g
and base degree
k_{\alpha}

f^{(g)}_{k_{\alpha}}(T)=\sum_{n\geq 0}{\rm GW}_{k_{\alpha}C^{\alpha}+nF}^{(g)}\,e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}T\left(n-\frac{\ell_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}k_{\beta}}{2N}\right)}

(2.44)

(where the sum over
n
is bounded from below)
such that

\mathcal{F}^{(g)}(T,S^{\alpha})=\mathcal{F}^{(g)}_{\rm pol}(T,S^{\alpha})+\sum_{k_{\alpha}\geq 0}f^{(g)}_{k_{\alpha}}(T)\,e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k_{\alpha}\widehat{S}^{\alpha}}

(2.45)

where the first term is the contribution of the non-constant polynomial terms that arise at genus zero and one.
In
[
24
,
26
,
25
]
, it was observed in a few examples that the generating series are quasimodular forms of weight
2g-2
. A more precise statement was conjectured for elliptic fibrations in
[
30
]
, and generalized to torus fibrations
in
[
33
]
,
13
13

13

Generalizations for elliptic fibrations with fibral divisors and/or non-zero Mordell-Weil rank have been proposed in
[
85
,
86
,
87
]
and were also extended to genus one fibrations in
[
33
]
.
in terms of the coefficients

Z_{k_{\alpha}}(T,\lambda)
appearing in the Fourier expansion
of the topological string partition function
Z_{\rm top}
with respect to
\widehat{S}^{\alpha}
, normalized by the base degree 0 contribution,

\frac{Z_{\rm top}(\widehat{S}^{\alpha},T,\lambda)}{Z_{0}(T,\lambda)}=1+\sum_{k_{\alpha}>0}Z_{k_{\alpha}}(T,\lambda)\,e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\widehat{S}^{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}\,.

(2.46)

It will be convenient to use the notations
Z_{k_{\alpha}}
and
Z_{H}
interchangeably, where

H=k_{\alpha}\check{D}^{\alpha}
is an effective divisor class on the base
B
.
Using (
2.18
),
Z_{H}(T,\lambda)
can equivalently be defined as the generating function of PT invariants with fixed base degree
H
,
normalized by the generating function of PT invariants with zero base degree,

\begin{gathered}Z_{k_{\alpha}}(T,\lambda)=\frac{\operatorname{PT}_{k_{\alpha}}(T,\lambda)}{\operatorname{PT}_{0}(T,\lambda)}\,,\\
\operatorname{PT}_{k_{\alpha}}(T,\lambda)=\sum_{n,m}\operatorname{PT}(k_{\alpha}C^{\alpha}+nF,m)\,e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(n-\frac{\ell_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}k_{\beta}}{2N})T+\mathrm{i}m\lambda}\,.\end{gathered}

(2.47)

For base degree zero, the only non-vanishing GV invariants are those listed in (
2.37
), so the GV/PT relation (
2.17
) gives

\begin{split}\operatorname{PT}_{0}(T,\lambda)=&\prod_{n>0}(1-e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}nT})^{-\chi_{B}}\\
\times&\prod_{\mu=0}^{N-1}\left(\prod_{k>0}\prod_{n>0}\left(1-(-1)^{k}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(\mu+nN)T+\mathrm{i}k\lambda}\right)^{(\mu+nN){\rm GV}^{(0)}_{\mu,0}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(2.48)

In terms of the normalized generating functions (
2.47
), the statement of
[
30
,
33
]
is that
Z_{H}(T,\lambda)
should be a meromorphic Jacobi form of weight 0 and index
h_{H}-1
under
the congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{1}(N)\subset SL(2,\mathds{Z})
defined in (
A
), where

h_{H}=1+\frac{1}{2}H\cap(H-c_{1}(B))=1+\frac{1}{2}k_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}(k_{\beta}-c_{\beta})

(2.49)

is the arithmetic genus of the curve class
H
.
This entails several properties of
Z_{H}(T,\check{\lambda})
that are most conveniently formulated using
\check{\lambda}:=\lambda/(2\pi)
and slightly abusing the notation:

i)

Periodicity under
\check{\lambda}\mapsto\check{\lambda}+1
and
T\mapsto T+1
(up to a phase), which is manifest from the definition (
2.47
).

ii)

Quasi-periodicity under
\check{\lambda}\mapsto\check{\lambda}+mNT
for any integer

,

Z_{H}(T,\check{\lambda}+mNT)=e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(h_{H}-1)(m^{2}NT+2m\check{\lambda})}Z_{H}(T,\check{\lambda})\,.

(2.50)

This was proven in
[
31
]
for elliptic fibrations (
N=1
) and reduced
14
14

14

I.e. for any decomposition
H=\sum H_{i}
into effective classes, all

H_{i}
’s are primitive.

H
, but remains conjectural in general. Note that one could rescale
\check{\lambda}\to\check{\lambda}/N
so as to enforce quasi-periodicity under
\check{\lambda}\to\check{\lambda}+mT
, at the expense of introducing fractional powers of

.

iii)

Modular invariance under the generator
\scriptsize\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\

of
\Gamma_{1}(N)
,

Z_{H}\left(\frac{T}{1+NT},\frac{\check{\lambda}}{1+NT}\right)=e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(h_{H}-1)\frac{\check{\lambda}^{2}}{1+NT}}Z_{H}(T,\check{\lambda})\,.

(2.51)

iv)

For
N\geq 5
, similar relations as (

) under the remaining generators of
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
Sets of generators for

can be found in Table

on page

.

We note that ii) follows from i) and iii), for which there is considerable evidence in explicit models but no mathematical proof yet. In fact, the conjectures in
[
30
,
33
]
go beyond these Jacobi modular properties, and provide an Ansatz specifying the analytic structure of

Z_{H}(T,\check{\lambda})
, namely

Z_{H}(T,\check{\lambda})=\frac{\Delta_{2N}(T)^{\frac{r_{H}}{N}}}{\eta(NT)^{12c_{1}(B)\cdot H}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{b_{2}(B)}\prod_{s=1}^{k_{\alpha}}\varphi_{-2,1}(NT,s\check{\lambda})}\,\varphi_{H}(T,\check{\lambda})

(2.52)

where
\eta(T)=q^{1/24}\prod(1-q^{n})
with
q:=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}T}
is the Dedekind eta function,
\Delta_{2N}(T)

is a specific modular form of weight
2N
under
\Gamma_{1}(N)
,
\varphi_{-2,1}(T,z)=-\vartheta_{1}(T,z)^{2}/\eta^{6}(T)
is a weak Jacobi form of weight
-2
and index 1,
and
\varphi_{H}(T,\check{\lambda})
is a weak Jacobi form of suitable weight and index,
15
15

15

For example, for an elliptic fibration over
B=\mathds{P}^{2}
, with hyperplane class
H
,
r_{nH}=0
and
\varphi_{nH}(T,\check{\lambda})
is a weak Jacobi form of weight
16n
and index
\frac{1}{3}n(n-1)(n+4)
.
such that
(
2.52
) has weight 0 and index
h_{H}-1
, as specified in (
2.49
).
The modular form
\Delta_{2N}(T)
is given explicitly by
16
16

16

See
[

, §D.4]
for a proof of the modularity properties of
\Delta_{2N}
.

\Delta_{2N}(T)=e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}T}\varphi_{-2,1}(NT,T)^{-N}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}q(1+8q+\ldots)&N=2\\
q^{N-1}\left(1+2Nq+\dots\right)&N\geq 3\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.53)

while the exponent
r_{H}
is determined modulo
N
by

r_{H}=\frac{1}{2}[N^{2}c_{1}(B)-\pi(D)]\cap H\text{ mod }N=\frac{1}{2}(N^{2}c_{\alpha}+\ell_{\alpha})C^{\alpha\beta}k_{\beta}\text{ mod }N\,,

(2.54)

Upon Laurent expanding around
\check{\lambda}=0
, these properties automatically imply that the generating series (

) are quasimodular forms of weight

under

, which furthermore satisfy
a holomorphic anomaly equation of the form

\begin{split}\partial_{E_{2}(NT)}f_{H}^{(g)}(T)=&-\frac{1}{24}\sum_{g=g_{1}+g_{2}\atop H=H_{1}+H_{2}}(H_{1}\cap H_{2})f_{H_{1}}^{(g_{1})}f_{H_{2}}^{(g_{2})}-\frac{1}{24}H\cap(H-c_{1}(B))\,f_{H}^{(g-1)}\\
\end{split}

(2.55)

where the second term is absent for

. For

, this recovers
[
25
]

[
32
, §3.6]
.

3
Base degree zero modularity and Eichler integrals

In this section, we derive general modular expressions for the base degree zero contributions
f^{(g)}_{0}(T)
to the topological string free energies, based on the simple structure of the vertical GV invariants summarized in (
2.37
).
This completes and generalizes the results from
[
30
, Section 4.5]
for
N=1
and
[
33
, Section 4.3]
for
N=2,3,4,6
to the case of genus one fibered Calabi-Yau threefolds with
N
-sections for arbitrary
N
. These results will also serve as consistency checks for the general transformation properties of the topological string partition function that will be the subject of Section
4
.

As reviewed in Section
2.4
, the only non-vanishing base degree zero Gopakumar-Vafa invariants are

\displaystyle\text{GV}^{(0)}_{mN,0_{\alpha}}=-\chi_{X}\,,\quad\text{GV}^{(0)}_{(m-1)N+a,0_{\alpha}}=\text{GV}^{(0)}_{mN-a,0_{\alpha}}=N_{a}\,,\quad\text{GV}^{(1)}_{mN,0_{\alpha}}=\chi_{B}\,,

(3.1)

for
m\geq 1
and
a\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor{\tiny\frac{N}{2}}\rfloor\}
.
The base degree zero contributions to the topological string free energies at arbitrary genus are then determined by the GV-formula (
2.9
), together with the expansion

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{q^{m}}{m}\left[2\sin\left(\frac{m\lambda}{2}\right)\right]^{-2}=\lambda^{-2}\text{Li}_{3}(q)+\sum\limits_{g\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{g+1}\lambda^{2g-2}B_{2g}}{2g[(2g-2)!]}\text{Li}_{3-2g}(q)\,,\end{split}

(3.2)

and the constant map contributions

\displaystyle F_{0}|_{\text{const.}}=-\frac{1}{2}\zeta(3)\chi_{X}\,,\quad F_{g\geq 2}|_{\text{const.}}=\frac{(-1)^{g-1}}{2}\frac{B_{2g}B_{2g-2}}{2g(2g-2)[(2g-2)!]}\chi_{X}\,.

(3.3)

At genus zero, this leads to the result

\displaystyle f^{(0)}_{0}(T)=-\chi_{X}\left(\frac{\zeta(3)}{2}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{3}(mNT)\right)+\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N-1}N_{k}\text{Li}_{3}\left(q^{mN+k}\right)\,,

(3.4)

while the higher genus free energies take the form

\displaystyle f_{0}^{(1)}(T)=\frac{1}{12}\left[(12\chi_{B}-\chi_{X})\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{1}(q^{mN})+\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N-1}N_{k}\,\text{Li}_{1}\left(q^{mN+k}\right)\right]\,,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{0}^{(g\geq 2)}(T)=&(-1)^{g+1}\frac{B_{2g}}{2g[(2g-2)!]}\left(\chi_{X}\left[\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}-\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{3-2g}\left(q^{mN}\right)\right]\right.\\
&\left.+\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N-1}N_{k}\,\text{Li}_{3-2g}\left(q^{mN+k}\right)\right)\,.\end{split}

(3.6)

3.1
Elliptic fibrations

Let us first understand the modular properties of these expressions (
3.4
), (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) in the easiest case
N=1
.
We start by recalling some well-known properties of Eisenstein series and their less familiar Eichler integrals.

For even
k\in\mathbb{N}
, with
k\geq 4
, the Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E_{k}(\tau):=1-\frac{2k}{B_{k}}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k-1}(n)q^{n}=1-\frac{2k}{B_{k}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{1-k}(q^{m})\,,

(3.7)

is a modular form of weight
k
for
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
.
The weight two Eisenstein series
E_{2}(\tau)
is a quasimodular form for
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
and transforms as
[
88
]

\displaystyle E_{2}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=(c\tau+d)^{2}E_{2}(\tau)+\frac{12}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}c(c\tau+d)\,,\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in SL(2,\mathds{Z})\,.

Using the relation

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sigma_{k-1}(m)q^{m}=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}n^{k-1}q^{m}=\sum\limits_{m,n\geq 1}n^{k-1}q^{mn}=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{1-k}(q^{m})\,,

(3.10)

one can rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{k}(\tau)=&1-\frac{2k}{B_{k}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{1-k}(q^{m})\,.\end{split}

(3.11)

In general, given a holomorphic modular form
f(\tau)=\sum_{n}a_{n}q^{n}
of weight
w
,
its
holomorphic Eichler integral
is defined as
\widetilde{f}(\tau)=P_{w-2}(\tau)+\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{a_{n}}{n^{w-1}}q^{n}
, such that the iterated derivative
(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{1-w}\partial_{\tau}^{w-1}=f(\tau)
. The
constant term

P_{w-1}(\tau)
is polynomial in
\tau
of degree
w-1
, unspecified except for its top degree term, determined by
a_{0}
. Under
\gamma\in SL(2,\mathds{Z})
,
\widetilde{f}(\tau)
transforms like up modular form of (negative) weight
2-w
, up to a
\gamma
-dependent polynomial of degree
w-2
(and a logarithmic term when
w=2
). We are particularly interested in the holomorphic Eichler integral of the weight four Eisenstein series
E_{4}(\tau)
, which we define following
[
89
, (27)]
(see also
[
90
, App. B]
and
[
91
,
92
]
)

\widetilde{E}_{-2}(\tau):=\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{1440}\tau^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\zeta(3)+\sum_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{3}(q^{m})\,,

(3.12)

such that

(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(\tau)=E_{4}(\tau)\,.

(3.13)

Under
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
,
E_{-2}(\tau)
transforms as a weight
-2
modular form, up to a quadratic polynomial in
\tau
,

\begin{split}\tau^{2}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(-1/\tau)=&\widetilde{E}_{-2}(\tau)+\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi^{3}}{36}\tau\,,\\
\widetilde{E}_{-2}(\tau+1)=&\widetilde{E}_{-2}(\tau)-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi^{3}}{180}(1+3\tau+3\tau^{2})\,.\end{split}

(3.14)

Similarly, the holomorphic Eichler integral of the weight two Eisenstein series
E_{2}(\tau)
takes the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{0}(\tau):=\log\eta(\tau)=\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{24}\tau-\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{1}(q^{m})\,,

(3.15)

and satisfies
(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{E}_{0}(\tau)=E_{2}(\tau)
.
The transformations of the Dedekind eta function

\displaystyle\eta(-1/\tau)=e^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\sqrt{\tau}\eta(\tau)\,,\quad\eta(\tau+1)=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}}\eta(\tau)\,,

(3.16)

imply that the Eichler integral transforms as

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{0}(-1/\tau)=\widetilde{E}_{0}(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\log(\tau)-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}\,,\quad\widetilde{E}_{0}(\tau+1)=\widetilde{E}_{0}(\tau)+\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\,.

(3.17)

Combining the expressions for the base degree zero free energies (
3.4
), (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), with (
3.12
), (
3.15
) and (
3.11
), and using
\chi_{X}=-60(12-\chi_{B})
, one obtains the identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{(0)}_{0}(T)=&-(12-\chi_{B})\left[\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{24}T^{3}+60\widetilde{E}_{-2}(T)\right]\,,\\
f^{(1)}_{0}(T)=&4(15-\chi_{B})\left[\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{24}T-\widetilde{E}_{0}(T)\right]\,,\\
f^{(g\geq 2)}_{0}(T)=&(-1)^{g}\frac{15B_{2g}B_{2g-2}}{g(2g-2)[(2g-2)!]}(12-\chi_{B})E_{2g-2}(T)\,.\end{split}

(3.18)

Further defining

\widehat{f}^{(0)}_{0}(T):=\frac{\widehat{\kappa}}{6}(2\pi{\rm i}T)^{3}+f^{(0)}_{0}(T),\quad\widehat{f}_{0}^{(1)}(T):=-\frac{\widehat{c}_{2}}{12}\pi{\rm i}T+f^{(1)}_{0}(T)

(3.19)

and using (
2.35
) with
N=1
, we see that the cubic and linear terms cancel, leading to

\widehat{f}^{(0)}_{0}(T)=-60(12-\chi_{B})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(T)\ ,\quad\widehat{f}^{(1)}_{0}(T)=-4(15-\chi_{B})\widetilde{E}_{0}(T)

(3.20)

The transformation properties of these expressions under
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
can be easily deduced from (
3.1
), (
3.14
) and (
3.17
). In particular, the third derivative
Y_{0}(T):=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-3}\partial_{T}^{3}\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}
(also known as Yukawa coupling) is a modular form of weight 4,
e^{\widehat{f}^{(1)}_{0}}
is a modular form of weight
2\chi_{B}-30
,
f^{(2)}
is a quasimodular form of weight 2
while
f^{(g\geq 3)}_{0}
is a modular form of weight
2g-2
.

3.2
Genus one fibrations

We shall now generalize the expressions (
3.18
) to genus one fibrations with
N
-sections.
To this end, it will be necessary to understand the modular properties of expressions

\displaystyle\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau):=\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}g_{N,a}(k)\text{Li}_{3-2g}\left(q^{mN+k}\right)\,,

(3.21)

where we use

\displaystyle g_{N,a}(k)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\text{ if }\,k\equiv\pm a\text{ mod }N\\
0&\text{ else}\end{array}\right.\,.

We relegate most of the technical work to
Appendices
A.2
–
A.4
.
In §
A.3
, we prove Lemma
4
, which together with Proposition
1
from §
A.2
implies that for
g\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{N,a}^{(g\geq 2)}(\tau):=-\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2g-2}\delta_{1,N/\gcd(N,a)}+\phi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)

(3.24)

is a modular form of weight
2g-2
for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
In Appendix
A.3
we also prove Lemma
5
, which implies that for
g\in\{0,1\}
the expression

\displaystyle\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau):=

\displaystyle-\frac{(2\pi{\rm i})^{3-2g}\beta_{4-2g,N,a}}{(4-2g)[(3-2g)!]}\tau^{3-2g}+\phi_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(\tau\right)\,,

(3.25)

is a holomorphic Eichler integral of a
\Gamma_{1}(N)
(quasi) modular form

\displaystyle\varphi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau):=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\Phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)\,,

(3.26)

of weight
4-2g
. For
g=\{0,1\}
, the coefficient
\beta_{4-2g,N,a}
defined
in (
A.11
) is given explicitly by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{2,N,a}=&\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{N^{2}}{6}-a(N-a)\right)\,,\quad\beta_{4,N,a}=\frac{1}{N}\left(-\frac{N^{4}}{30}+a^{2}(N-a)^{2}\right)\,.\end{split}

(3.27)

The transformation properties of
\Phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)
under
\tau\rightarrow\tau+1
and
\tau\rightarrow\tau/(N\tau+1)
are determined in Appendix
A.4
and summarized in Theorem
1
on page
1
.

Genus 0.

Using (
3.12
) and (
3.25
), we can express (
3.4
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{(0)}_{0}(T)=&(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}CT^{3}-\chi_{X}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(NT)+\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\Phi^{(0)}_{N,a}(T)\,,\end{split}

(3.28)

where the constant
C
is defined as

\displaystyle C:=

\displaystyle\frac{\chi_{X}}{1440}N^{3}+\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\frac{N_{a}}{24N}\left(-\frac{N^{4}}{30}+a^{2}(N-a)^{2}\right)\,.

(3.29)

Using (
2.38
) and (
2.36
), we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\begin{split}C=&\frac{N^{3}}{1440}\left(\chi_{X}-\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\right)+\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N-1}\frac{N_{a}}{24N}a^{2}(N-a)^{2}=-\frac{1}{6}\widehat{\kappa}\,,\end{split}

(3.30)

In terms of the function
\widehat{f}^{(0)}_{0}
introduced in (
3.19
),
we therefore have

\displaystyle\widehat{f}^{(0)}_{0}(T)=-\chi_{X}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(NT)+\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\Phi^{(0)}_{N,a}(T)\,.

(3.31)

Using Theorem
1
and (
3.14
), as well as (
2.38
), we see that this transforms as

\displaystyle\begin{split}(NT+1)^{2}&\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}\left(\frac{T}{NT+1}\right)-\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)\\
=&\frac{{\rm i}\pi^{3}}{180}\chi_{X}(4N^{2}T^{2}-3NT-3)\\
&-\frac{{\rm i}\pi^{3}}{90}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\left[2(15a^{2}-15aN+2N^{2})T^{2}-3NT-3\right]\\
=&(2\pi{\rm i})^{3}\left[\frac{1}{8}(\chi_{B}-12)(NT+1)+\frac{1}{24}N(\widehat{c}_{2}-12N)T^{2}\right]\,,\end{split}

(3.32)

as well as
\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T+1)-\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}\,\widehat{\kappa}\,(3T^{2}+3T+1)/6
.
The third derivative of
\hat{f}^{(0)}(T)
is a linear combination of
\Gamma_{1}(N)
Eisenstein series of weight four,

\displaystyle Y_{0}(T):=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-3}\partial_{T}^{3}\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)=-\chi_{X}N^{3}E_{4}(N\tau)+\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\varphi^{(0)}_{N,a}(T)\,.

(3.33)

More generally, under
\gamma=\scriptsize\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{1}(N)
,
\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}
transforms as

\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}\left(\frac{aT+b}{cT+d}\right)=\frac{\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)+xT^{2}+yT+z}{(cT+d)^{2}}

(3.34)

where
x,y,z
are
\gamma
-dependent constants, subject to obvious cocycles relations, such that the third derivative transforms as
Y_{0}\left(\frac{aT+b}{cT+d}\right)=(cT+d)^{4}Y_{0}(T)
.

Genus 1.

Using (
3.12
) and (
3.25
), we can express (
3.5
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{(1)}_{0}(T)=&2\pi\mathrm{i}DT-\left(\chi_{B}-\frac{\chi_{X}}{12}\right)\widetilde{E}_{0}(NT)+\frac{1}{12}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\Phi^{(1)}_{N,a}(T)\,,\end{split}

(3.35)

where the constant
D
is defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}D:=&\frac{N}{24}\left(\chi_{B}-\frac{\chi_{X}}{12}\right)+\frac{1}{24}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\frac{N_{a}}{N}\left(\frac{N^{2}}{6}-a(N-a)\right)\,.\end{split}

(3.36)

Using also (
2.38
) and (
2.36
), we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\begin{split}D=&\frac{N}{24}\left(\chi_{B}+5(12-\chi_{B})\right)-\frac{1}{24N}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}a(N-a)=\frac{\widehat{c}_{2}}{24}\,,\end{split}

(3.37)

In terms of the function
\widehat{f}^{(1)}_{0}
introduced in (
3.19
),
it follows that

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{0}^{(1)}(T)=\left(\frac{\chi_{X}}{12}-\chi_{B}\right)\widetilde{E}_{0}(NT)+\frac{1}{12}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\Phi^{(1)}_{N,a}(T)\,.

(3.38)

Using Theorem
1
and (
3.17
), as well as (
2.38
) and (
2.36
), we find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{f}_{0}^{(1)}\left(\tfrac{T}{NT+1}\right)&-\widehat{f}_{0}^{(1)}(T)\\
=&\left(\frac{\chi_{X}}{12}-\chi_{B}\right)\left[\frac{1}{2}\log(NT+1)-\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{12}\right]-\frac{1}{12}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\hat{c}_{N}^{(1)}\\
=&\frac{1}{24}\left(\chi_{X}-12\chi_{B}\right)\log(NT+1)+5\pi{\rm i}-\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{3}\chi_{B}\,,\end{split}

(3.39)

as well as
\widehat{f}_{0}^{(1)}(T+1)-\widehat{f}_{0}^{(1)}(T)=-\pi\mathrm{i}\widehat{c}_{2}/12
.
More generally, under
\gamma=\scriptsize\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{1}(N)
,
e^{\widehat{f}_{0}^{(1)}}
transforms as a modular form of weight
\frac{\chi_{X}}{24}-\frac{\chi_{B}}{2}
, up to a phase subject to the group relations.
Moreover, the derivative of
\widehat{f}^{(1)}(T)
is a quasimodular form of weight two under
\Gamma_{1}(N)
,

\displaystyle(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\partial_{T}\widehat{f}^{(1)}(T)=N\left(\chi_{B}-\frac{\chi_{X}}{12}\right)E_{2}(NT)+\frac{1}{12}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\varphi^{(1)}_{N,a}(T)\,.

(3.40)

Genus
g\geq 2
.

Using (
A.41
) and (
3.21
), we can express (
3.6
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f^{(g\geq 2)}_{0}(T)\\
=&(-1)^{g+1}\frac{B_{2g}}{2g[(2g-2)!]}\left[\chi_{X}\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}E_{2g-2}(NT)+\sum\limits_{a=1}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}N_{a}\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(T)\right]\,.\end{split}

(3.41)

For
g=2
, one can use Lemma
4
, together with the fact that
e_{N,2}(\tau):=NE_{2}(N\tau)-E_{2}(\tau)
is a modular form of weight two for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
, to see that
f^{(2)}_{0}(T)
itself is a quasimodular form of weight two for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
For
g\geq 3
, Lemma
4
implies that
f^{(g)}_{0}(T)
is a modular form of weight
2g-2
for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.

3.3
Simplified formulae for
N=2,3,4,6

For
N=2,3,4,6
, the relevant Eisenstein series of
\Gamma_{1}(N)
can all be obtained in terms of the Eisenstein series of
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
with arguments rescaled by divisors of
N
. In this way,
we get, for
N=2
,

\begin{split}\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)=&N_{1}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(T)-(\chi_{X}+N_{1})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(2T)\,,\\
\widehat{f}^{(1)}_{0}(T)=&-\frac{N_{1}}{12}\log\eta(T)+\frac{N_{1}+\chi_{X}-4\chi_{B}}{12}\log\eta(2T)\,,\end{split}

(3.42)

for
N=3
,

\begin{split}\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)=&N_{1}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(T)-(\chi_{X}+N_{1})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(3T)\,,\\
\widehat{f}^{(1)}_{0}(T)=&-\frac{N_{1}}{12}\log\eta(T)+\frac{N_{1}+\chi_{X}-4\chi_{B}}{12}\log\eta(3T)\,,\end{split}

(3.43)

for
N=4
,

\begin{split}\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)=&N_{1}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(T)+(N_{2}-N_{1})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(2T)-(\chi_{X}+N_{2})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(4T)\,,\\
\widehat{f}^{(1)}_{0}(T)=&-\frac{N_{1}}{12}\log\eta(T)+\frac{N_{1}-N_{2}}{12}\log\eta(2T)+\frac{N_{2}+\chi_{X}-4\chi_{B}}{12}\log\eta(4T)\,,\end{split}

(3.44)

and for
N=6

\begin{split}\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0)}(T)=&N_{1}\widetilde{E}_{-2}(T)+(N_{2}-N_{1})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(2T)\\
&+(N_{3}-N_{1})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(3T)-(\chi_{X}-N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{3})\widetilde{E}_{-2}(6T)\,,\\
\widehat{f}^{(1)}_{0}(T)=&-\frac{N_{1}}{12}\log\eta(T)+\frac{N_{1}-N_{2}}{12}\log\eta(2T)+\frac{N_{1}-N_{3}}{12}\log\eta(3T)\\
&+\frac{N_{2}+N_{3}-N_{1}+\chi_{X}-4\chi_{B}}{12}\log\eta(6T)\,.\end{split}

(3.45)

For
N=5,6
, the transformation properties of
\widehat{f}_{0}^{(0,1)}
under the extra generator of
\Gamma_{1}(N)
(see point iv) in §
2.4
) can be obtained from the transformation properties of the Eichler integrals conjectured in §
A.4.1
.

4
Modularity from wave-function property

In this section, we shall derive the modular properties of generating functions of GW invariants, and the Jacobi properties of PT invariants, from the wave-function behavior of the topological string partition function under a suitable monodromy.

4.1
Wave-function property of the topological string partition function

First, let us recall the wave function interpretation of the topological string partition function, following
[
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
93
]
. This interpretation is most transparent in terms of the topological B-model, which depends only on the complex structure of the Calabi-Yau manifold, rather than the topological A-model, which depends only on the complexified Kähler moduli, but the two are related by mirror symmetry. Thus, the moduli
t^{a}
below stand for flat coordinates on the complex structure moduli space

\mathcal{M}
of the CY threefold
\widehat{X}
related to
X
by mirror symmetry. With this in mind, let us define
17
17

17

In
[
2
]
,
\chi
should be understood as the Euler number of the A-model geometry,
\chi=\chi_{X}=-\chi_{\widehat{X}}
.

\Psi_{\rm BCOV}(t,\bar{t};\lambda,x)=\lambda^{\frac{\chi_{X}}{24}-1}\exp\left(\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\lambda^{2g-2}\ C^{(g)}_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}(t,\bar{t})\,x^{a_{1}}\cdots x^{a_{n}}\right).

(4.1)

where
C^{(g)}_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
are the topological correlators, which
vanish unless
2g-2+n>0
. Those arise as iterated covariant derivatives of the
genus
g
free-energies
\mathcal{F}^{(g)}(t,\bar{t})
for
g\geq 1
, or of the
Yukawa couplings
C_{abc}
for
g=0
. The topological string partition function (
4.1
)
satisfies the holomorphic anomaly equations
[
2
, (3.17-18)]

\begin{split}\left[\partial_{\bar{t}^{a}}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}e^{2\mathcal{K}}\bar{C}_{\bar{a}\bar{b}\bar{c}}g^{b\bar{b}}g^{c\bar{c}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{b}\partial x^{c}}+g_{\bar{a}b}x^{b}\left(\lambda\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}+x^{a}\partial_{x^{a}}\right)\right]\Psi_{\rm BCOV}=0,\\
\left[\partial_{t^{a}}+\Gamma_{ab}^{c}x^{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{c}}+\partial_{t^{a}}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{\chi_{X}}{24}-1-\lambda\partial_{\lambda}\right)-\partial_{x^{a}}+\partial_{t^{a}}\mathcal{F}_{1}+\frac{1}{2\lambda^{2}}C_{abc}x^{b}x^{c}\right]\Psi_{\rm BCOV}=0,\end{split}

(4.2)

where
\Gamma_{ab}^{c}
are Levi-Civita coefficients of the special Kähler metric
g_{a\bar{b}}\mathrm{d}t^{a}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}^{\bar{b}}

with Kähler potential
\mathcal{K}(t,\bar{t})
. As explained in
[
42
,
43
]
, these
equations naturally arise by viewing
\Psi_{\rm BCOV}
as the wave function of a particular
state
|\Psi_{\rm top}\rangle
in the Hilbert space obtained by quantizing the symplectic space
H^{3}(\widehat{X},\mathds{R})
, in a suitable complex polarization determined by the complex structure on
\widehat{X}
.

More precisely, the real vector space
H^{3}(\widehat{X},\mathds{R})
carries a symplectic form

\omega(C,C^{\prime})

=\int_{\widehat{X}}C\wedge C^{\prime}
, and inherits a complex structure (called Griffith’s complex structure) from the Hodge decomposition

C=x^{0}\Omega_{3,0}+x^{a}D_{a}\Omega_{3,0}+\bar{x}^{\bar{a}}D_{\bar{a}}\bar{\Omega}_{3,0}+\bar{x}^{0}\bar{\Omega}_{3,0}\,,

(4.3)

where
\Omega_{3,0}
is a nowhere vanishing holomorphic 3-form on
\widehat{X}
, depending
on the moduli
(t,\bar{t})
. In the complex coordinates
x^{\Lambda}=(x^{0},x^{a})
, the symplectic form becomes

\omega=e^{-\mathcal{K}}\left(g_{a\bar{b}}\mathrm{d}x^{a}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{x}^{\bar{b}}-\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{x}^{0}\right)=\mathrm{d}x^{\Lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}_{\Lambda}

(4.4)

where
\tilde{x}_{\Lambda}:=(\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{a}):=e^{-\mathcal{K}}(-\bar{x}^{0},g_{a\bar{b}}\bar{x}^{\bar{b}})
.
Upon quantization, the Darboux coordinates
(x^{\Lambda},\tilde{x}_{\Lambda})
become operators
18
18

18

Note the unusual hermiticity property
\tilde{x}^{0}=-e^{-\mathcal{K}}\bar{x}_{0}
,
which originates from the minus sign in (
4.4
) and leads to convergence issues. This can be remedied by
exchanging
(x^{0},\bar{x}^{0})
, which amounts to using Weil’s complex structure rather
than Griffith’s, see
[
45
,
46
]
.

satisfying the canonical commutation rules

[\widehat{x^{\Lambda}},\widehat{\tilde{x}_{\Sigma}}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\,\delta^{\Lambda}_{\Sigma}\,,\quad[\widehat{x^{\Lambda}},\widehat{x^{\Sigma}}]=[\widehat{\tilde{x}_{\Lambda}},\widehat{\tilde{x}_{\Sigma}}]=0\,.

(4.5)

Introducing a basis of (dual) coherent states
{}_{z,\bar{z}}\langle x^{\Lambda}|

which diagonalize the operators
\widehat{x^{\Lambda}}
, the topological string partition function can be interpreted as the overlap

\Psi_{\rm BCOV}(t,\bar{t};\lambda,x)=_{t,\bar{t}}\langle x^{\Lambda}|\Psi_{\rm top}\rangle,\quad

(4.6)

where
|\Psi_{\rm top}\rangle
is a fixed, background independent state
(as will become clear below, the topological string coupling
\lambda
can be identified with
\sqrt{\hbar}
in the Kähler gauge
X^{0}=1
).
The indices
t,\bar{t}
indicate that the Hodge decomposition (
4.3
) depends in a non-holomorphic way on the moduli

t^{a}
. The resulting dependence of the coherent states implies the holomorphic anomaly equations (
4.2
).

On the other hand, after choosing a symplectic basis
(g^{\Lambda},\tilde{g}_{\Lambda})
of
H^{3}(\widehat{X},\mathds{Z})
(also known as marking, satisfying
\int_{X}g^{\Lambda}\wedge\tilde{g}_{\Sigma}=\delta^{\Lambda}_{\Sigma}
,
\int_{X}g^{\Lambda}\wedge g^{\Sigma}=\int_{X}\tilde{g}_{\Lambda}\wedge\tilde{g}_{\Sigma}=0
), one can alternatively parametrize
V

by real Darboux coordinates
(\zeta^{\Lambda},\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda})
,

C=\zeta^{\Lambda}a_{\Lambda}-\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda}b^{\Lambda}\ ,\quad\omega=\mathrm{d}\zeta^{\Lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda}

(4.7)

such that
(\zeta^{\Lambda},\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda})
become hermitean operators satisfying the canonical
commutation rules

[\widehat{\zeta^{\Lambda}},\widehat{\tilde{\zeta}_{\Sigma}}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\,\delta^{\Lambda}_{\Sigma}\,,\quad[\widehat{\zeta^{\Lambda}},\widehat{\zeta^{\Sigma}}]=[\widehat{\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda}},\widehat{\tilde{\zeta}_{\Sigma}}]=0

(4.8)

and work in a polarization
where the hermitean operator
\widehat{\zeta^{\Lambda}}
is diagonalized, while
\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda}=\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{\zeta^{\Lambda}}
. The resulting ‘real polarized’
wave function
\Psi_{\mathds{R}}(\zeta^{\Lambda})
no longer depends on a choice of background
(z,\bar{z})
, but it depends on a choice of marking. Under monodromies in
\mathcal{M}
, it transforms under the metaplectic representation of
Sp(b_{3}(\widehat{X}),\mathds{Z})
.
If the monodromy acts via

\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda}\\
\zeta^{\Lambda}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\zeta}_{\Lambda}\\
\zeta^{\Lambda}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(4.9)

where
A,B,C,D
are square matrices of size
\frac{1}{2}b_{3}(\widehat{X})
with integer entries satisfying (
2.3
),
and if the matrix
C
is invertible, the wave function should transform as

\Psi_{\mathds{R}}(\zeta^{\Lambda})\mapsto\Psi^{\prime}_{\mathds{R}}(\zeta^{\prime\Lambda})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\,{\rm det}\,(\hbar C)}}\int e^{-S(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})/\hbar}\Psi_{\mathds{R}}(\zeta^{\Lambda})\,\prod_{\Lambda}\mathrm{d}\zeta^{\Lambda}

(4.10)

where, similar to (
2.5
),

S(\zeta^{\Lambda},\zeta^{\prime\Lambda})=-\frac{1}{2}\zeta^{t}C^{-1}D\zeta+\zeta^{t}C^{-1}\zeta^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\zeta^{\prime t}AC^{-1}\zeta^{\prime}\,.

(4.11)

If the matrix
C
is not invertible, the Gaussian kernel should be replaced by the product of a delta function along the null directions of
C
, times a Gaussian kernel along its orthogonal complement.
For example, if
C=0
and therefore
D=A^{-T}
,
\Psi^{\prime}_{\mathds{R}}(\zeta^{\prime\Lambda})=e^{\frac{1}{2}\zeta^{\prime}B\zeta^{\prime}}\Psi_{\mathds{R}}(D^{-1}\zeta^{\prime})
.

The key question is now to relate the real polarized wave function
\Psi_{\mathds{R}}(\zeta)
to the BCOV wave function
\Psi_{\rm BCOV}(t,\bar{t};\lambda,x)
. For this, following
[
93
]
, we define the quasi-homogeneous function

\begin{split}\widetilde{\Psi}(t,\bar{t},\lambda;x^{\Lambda}):=&\lambda^{1-\frac{\chi_{X}}{24}}\Psi_{\rm BCOV}(z,\bar{z};\lambda/x^{0},x^{a}/x^{0})\\
