, (5.33)]
,
by noting that higher-derivative couplings of the form
c_{\alpha}\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}\wedge\mathcal{R}\wedge\mathcal{R}
term in 5
dimensions (where
\mathcal{R}
is the Riemann tensor and
\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}
the graviphotons) induce a shift
q_{\alpha}\mapsto q_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{8}c_{2\alpha}
, which amounts at leading order to shifting
C^{2}\mapsto C^{2}+3c_{1}(B)\cdot C
as in the central charge
c_{L}
in (
5.51
).

The upshot of this discussion is that the central charge (
5.51
) appears to control the growth of indices counting 5D black hole in the Cardy limit, or PT indices counting 4D black holes with one unit of D6-brane charge, but that the growth of indices counting D4-D2-D0 black holes wrapping the genus-one fiber is instead controlled by the MSW central charge (
5.45
), even though the pulled back divisor is not ample but only nef. It would be very interesting to reproduce the full black hole entropy (
5.55
) from microscopic counting.

6
Discussion and open problems

In this work, we have revisited the modular properties of generating series of various enumerative invariants of torus-fibered Calabi-Yau threefolds, restricting for simplicity to fibrations with trivial Mordell-Weil group and no fibral divisors (hence at most discrete gauge symmetries in the corresponding F-theory vacua, see
[
40
]
). Our main result is a derivation of the Jacobi property of the generating series of PT invariants at fixed base degree, assuming the wave function property of the topological string partition function
Z_{\rm top}(t^{a},\lambda)
under a suitable monodromy. This wave function property is physically well motivated, but far from being established mathematically, and our work can be viewed as further evidence for its validity for general projective CY threefolds (see e.g.
[
102
,
103
,
104
]
for rigorous results in this direction for toric CY singularities and
[
105
]
for a proof of the BCOV equations for the quintic).

While we have only considered
Z_{\rm top}(t^{a},\lambda)
(or rather its logarithm) as an asymptotic series in the topological string coupling
\lambda
, the wave-function property is expected to hold for the putative non-perturbative completion of the topological string amplitude, potentially fixing it uniquely, and it would be extremely interesting to understand its consequences for the resurgent structure of the asymptotic series, along the lines
of
[
106
,
107
,
108
]
.

Notably, a key step in our derivation of the Jacobi properties was to introduce a variant
Z_{\rm mod}(t^{a},\lambda)
of the topological string amplitude, which is both holomorphic and modular invariant, up to a multiplier system entirely determined by the topological data of the fibration. This was made possible by the introduction of the improved Kähler moduli
\widetilde{S}^{\alpha}
(
4.54
), which transform simply by an additive constant under
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
As an asymptotic series in
\lambda
,
Z_{\rm mod}
is essentially determined by the depth-zero part of the quasimodular series of GW invariants at arbitrary degree and genus. A better understanding of its analytic properties might open the way to determine all-genus GV invariants at higher degrees than currently available.

On the other hand, our derivation heavily hinges on the structure (
3.1
) of the base degree zero GV invariants, as well as on the relations (
2.36
)
to the intersection numbers of the fibrations. In the elliptic case, i.e.
N=1
, the relations (
3.1
) follow from
[
83
, Theorem 6.9]
, the proof of which
relies on the fact that for elliptic fibrations, the smooth generic Weierstraß fibration is isomorphic to the corresponding relative Jacobian fibration. We expect that the relations for
N>1
can be proven along the same lines but using the twisted derived equivalence of the smooth genus one fibered Calabi-Yau with its relative Jacobian fibration
[
54
, Theorem 5.1]
. Clearly, it would be interesting to generalize our results to general fibrations with fibral divisors and/or non-trivial Mordell-Weil group (see
[
109
]
for new results in this direction).

It was proposed in
[
35
]
and further elaborated on in
[
41
,
40
]
, that the
\Gamma_{1}(N)
-modular properties of
Z_{\rm top}
extend to vector valued modular properties under the full modular group, when taking into account the topological string partition functions on all genus one fibered Calabi-Yau threefolds that share the same Jacobian fibration.
This requires including suitable flat but topologically non-trivial B-field backgrounds on fibrations that exhibit
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminal singularities, see also
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
.
Lemma
2
, together with Lemma
4
and
5
, can be used to prove this vector valued modularity for the vertical invariants under the Fricke involution
T\rightarrow-1/(NT)
, assuming the Ansatz for the topological string partition function in the presence of a flat but topologically non-trivial B-field in terms of torsion refined Gopakumar-Vafa invariants from
[
35
]
.
More generally, it should be possible to generalize these results to understand the full vector valued modularity for the vertical invariants and to use the wave function property of the topological string partition function to extend this beyond base degree zero.

Our second main result was to relate the generating series of genus zero Gromov-Witten invariants at fixed base degree
H
(which, up to multi-cover effects, count BPS bound states of D2-D0 branes wrapped on
H+nF
) to the generating series of rank 0 rational DT invariants supported on the pulled-back divisor
D=\pi^{-1}(H)
(which, up to different multi-cover effects, count BPS bound states of D4-D2-D0 branes wrapped on
D
). Restricting for simplicity to genus one fibrations over
\mathds{P}^{2}
, the proposed relation (
5.22
) (extending the earlier proposal of
[
26
]
to the case of genus one fibrations with
N
-sections)
arises by following DT invariants under the same relative conifold monodromy, assuming the absence of wall-crossing. It is clearly an important problem to prove this assumption, and extend the relation (
5.22
) to more general torus fibrations.
If this relation is correct, the fact that the generating series
f_{H}
are quasimodular forms under
\Gamma_{1}(N)
implies that the generating series
h_{D,\mu}
transform as
vector-valued mock modular forms under
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
, exactly as predicted in
[
15
,
14
]
, given the fact that for isotropic charge vectors, mock modularity reduces to quasimodularity. This is similar to the case of K3-fibrations, where the modularity of the generating series of vertical rank 0 DT invariants can be reduced to the modularity of generating series of Noether-Lefschetz invariants
[
23
,
22
]
.

Clearly, both in the case of torus and K3-fibrations, it would be very interesting to compute rank 0 DT invariants for general divisor classes with non-trivial support on the base, so as to provide further support to the S-duality conjectures, and possibly obtain new constraints on higher genus GV invariants, along the lines of
[
12
,
13
]
. While it is known that in the case of a smooth elliptic fibration over a del Pezzo surface
B
, rank 0 DT invariants supported on
For
[D]=r[B]
coincide with rank
r
Vafa-Witten invariants on
B

[
110
,
111
,
112
]
, and therefore have modular generating series, it is an interesting problem to generalize this to the case of torus fibrations with
N
-sections. Similarly, it would be interesting to clarify the relation (if any) between GV invariants supported on the basis of a torus fibration with
N
-section, and GV invariants of the non-compact CY threefold given by the total space of the canonnical bundle
K_{B}
.
We hope to return to some of these questions in future work.

Appendix A
Modular forms for
\Gamma_{1}(N)

In this appendix, we review basic facts about modular forms for the congruence group
\Gamma_{1}(N)
, introduce Eisenstein series with Dirichlet character
and their holomorphic Eichler integrals,
and obtain new results for their transformation under Fricke involutions.
The results are used in Section
3
to understand the modular properties of the base degree zero contributions to the topological string free energies of a generic genus one fibered CY threefold.

The congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{1}(N)\subset SL(2,\mathds{Z})
is defined as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(N):=\left\{\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in SL(2,\mathds{Z})\,\,\bigg|\,\,a,d\equiv 1\text{ mod }N\,,\quad c\equiv 0\text{ mod }N\,\right\}\,.

For
1\leq N\leq 4
, the group
\Gamma_{1}(N)
is generated by the two elements
\tiny\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
and
\tiny\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
N&1\end{array}\right)
.
More generally, the minimal number of generators is given by
2g(N)+n_{c}(N)-3
in terms of the genus
g(N)
and the number of cusps
n_{c}(N)
of the modular curve
X_{1}(N)=\mathbb{H}/\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
We have listed sets of generators for
N=5,6,7
in Table
1
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{|c|c|}\hline\cr N&\text{Generators of }\Gamma_{1}(N)\\
\hline\cr 5&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
5&1\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}11&-4\\
25&-9\\
\end{array}\right)\\
6&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
6&1\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}7&-3\\
12&-5\\
\end{array}\right)\\
7&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
7&1\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}15&-4\\
49&-13\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-13&5\\
-21&8\\
\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}22&-9\\
49&-20\\
\end{array}\right)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 1:
Generators of the group
\Gamma_{1}(N)
for
5\leq N\leq 7
, obtained using the algorithm described in
[
113
, Section 1.4]
and implemented in
Sage

[
114
]
with the command
Gamma1(N).generators()
.

We denote the corresponding ring of modular forms by
M(N)
and let
M_{w}(N)\subset M(N)
be the subspace of modular forms of weight
w
.
Since
\Gamma_{1}(1)=\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, we have
M(1)=\langle E_{4},E_{6}\rangle
in terms of the Eisenstein series
E_{4}(\tau)
,
E_{6}(\tau)
.
Using (
3.1
), it is easy to see that

\displaystyle e_{N,2}(\tau):=NE_{2}(N\tau)-E_{2}(\tau)\,,

(A.3)

is a modular form of weight
2
for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
A basis for the ring of modular forms for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
with
N\leq 5
have been constructed for example in
[
35
, Appendix B]
and we briefly summarize the relevant forms in Table
2
.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr N&\mbox{Generators of }M(N)&\Delta_{2N}\\
\hline\cr 1&e_{1,4}=E_{4},e_{1,6}=E_{6}&\\
\hline\cr\hbox{\multirowsetup 2}&e_{2,2}=2E_{2}(2\tau)-E_{2}(\tau)=1+24q+24q^{2}+96q^{3}+24q^{4}+\ldots&\frac{e_{2,4}-e_{2,4}^{2}}{192}\\
&e_{2,4}=E_{4}&\\
\hline\cr\hbox{\multirowsetup 3}&e_{3,1}=\left(\frac{\eta(\tau)^{9}}{\eta(3\tau)^{3}}+27\frac{\eta(3\tau)^{9}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}=1+6q+6q^{3}+6q^{4}+\ldots&e_{3,3}^{2}\\
&e_{3,3}=\frac{\eta(3\tau)^{9}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}=q+3q^{2}+9q^{3}+13q^{4}+\ldots&\\
\hline\cr\hbox{\multirowsetup 4}&e_{4,1}=\frac{\eta(2\tau)^{10}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}\eta(4\tau)^{4}}=1+4q+4q^{2}+4q^{4}+\ldots&-\frac{e_{4,1}^{2}(e_{4,1}^{2}-e_{2,2})^{3}}{4096}\\
&e_{2,2}&\\
\hline\cr\hbox{\multirowsetup 5}&e_{5,1}=\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}^{2}}{\left((q;q^{5})_{\infty}(q^{4};q^{5})_{\infty}\right)^{5}}=1+3q+4q^{2}+2q^{3}+q^{4}+\ldots&e_{5,1}^{6}\tilde{e}_{5,1}^{4}\\
&\tilde{e}_{5,1}=\frac{q\,(q;q)_{\infty}^{2}}{\left((q^{2};q^{5})_{\infty}(q^{3};q^{5})_{\infty}\right)^{5}}=q-2q^{2}+4q^{3}-3q^{4}+\ldots&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 2:
Generators of the ring
M(N)
of modular forms for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
with
N\leq 5
, and canonical modular form
\Delta_{2N}
for
2\leq N\leq 5
. We denote by
e_{N,w}
or
e_{N,w,\bullet}
a modular form for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
of weight
w
and
(a;q)_{n}
is the
q
-Pochhammer symbol
(a;q)_{n}=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^{k})
.

A.1
Dirichlet characters, Bernoulli numbers and L-series

Let us first recall some definitions and properties of Dirichlet characters, generalized Bernoulli numbers and Dirichlet L-series from
[
115
, Chapter 3]
.

We denote the group of Dirichlet characters with modulus
N
by

\displaystyle D[N]:=\widehat{\left(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}\right)^{\times}}\,.

(A.4)

Since
\chi(-1)^{2}=\chi(1)=1
, we have that
\chi(-1)\in\{-1,1\}
.
We denote by
D[N]^{\pm}\subset D[N]
the subgroup of Dirichlet characters with modulus
N
and
\chi(-1)=\pm 1
.
Given a character
\chi
with modulus
N
, the conductor
m_{\chi}\in\mathbb{N}
of
\chi
is the smallest positive integer such that
\chi(m)=\chi(n)
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\gcd(m,N)=\gcd(n,N)=1
and
m\equiv n\text{ mod }m_{\chi}
.
The conductor always divides the modulus,
m_{\chi}|N
.

A character
\chi\in D[N]
is called primitive if
N=m_{\chi}
.
If
\chi
is not primitive, there is a unique primitive character
\chi^{\star}\in D[m_{\chi}]
, such that

\displaystyle\chi(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\chi^{\star}(n)&\text{ if }\gcd(n,N)=1\\
0&\text{ if }\gcd(n,N)\neq 1\\
\end{array}\right.\,.

The so-called principal character
\chi_{N,0}
is given by

\displaystyle\chi_{N,0}(n):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if }\gcd(n,N)\neq 1\\
1&\text{ if }\gcd(n,N)=1\\
\end{array}\right.\,,

and always has conductor
1
. The associated primitive character is the trivial character
\chi\in D[1]
with
\chi(n)=1
.
The number of Dirichlet characters of modulus
N
is given by Euler’s totient function

\displaystyle\varphi(N)=N\prod\limits_{p|N}\left(1-p^{-1}\right)\,,

(A.9)

where the product is over prime factors of
N
.

The generalized Bernoulli numbers
B_{k,\chi}
are defined via the relation
29
29

29

Compared to
[
115
]
, we extend the definition of
B_{k,\chi}

to the case that
\chi
is not necessarily primitive.

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(a)\frac{xe^{ax}}{e^{Nx}-1}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}B_{k,\chi}\frac{x^{k}}{k!}\,.

(A.10)

Expanding the left-hand side of (
A.10
) in
x
, we can introduce
\beta_{k,\chi,a}
via the relation

\displaystyle B_{k,\chi}=\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(a)\beta_{k,N,a}\,,\quad\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{k,N,a}\frac{x^{k}}{k!}=\frac{xe^{ax}}{e^{Nx}-1}\,,

(A.11)

and note that
\beta_{k,rN,ra}=r^{k-1}\beta_{k,N,a}
.
We have the relation

\displaystyle B_{k,\chi}=

\displaystyle B_{k,\chi^{\star}}\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)d^{k-1}\chi^{\star}(d)=B_{k,\chi^{\star}}\prod\limits_{p|N}\left(1-p^{k-1}\chi^{\star}(p)\right)\,,

(A.12)

in terms of the Möbius function

\displaystyle\mu(n)=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\text{ if }\,n=1\\
(-1)^{k}&\text{ if $n$ has $k$ distinct prime factors}\\
0&\text{ if $n$ is divisible by a square greater than one}\end{array}\right.\,.

The Dirichlet L-series is defined as

\displaystyle L(s,\chi)=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\chi(n)}{n^{s}}\,.

(A.16)

One has the relation

\displaystyle L(s,\chi)=L(s,\chi^{\star})\prod\limits_{p|N}\left(1-\frac{\chi^{\star}(p)}{p^{s}}\right)\,,

(A.17)

where the product is over the prime factors of
N
.
If
\chi\in D[N]^{+}
(
\chi\in D[N]^{-}
), the only zeros
L(s,\chi)
with
s\in\mathbb{Z}
are simple zeros at the even (odd) negative integers.
We will also need that

\displaystyle L(0,\chi^{\star})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if }\,m_{\chi}>1\\
-\frac{1}{2}&\text{ if }\,m_{\chi}=1\end{array}\right.\,.

If
\chi
is a primitive character with modulus
N>1
, and
\delta\in\{0,1\}
satisfies
\chi(-1)=(-1)^{\delta}
, then
L(s,\chi)
satisfies the functional equation

\displaystyle L(s,\chi)=W(\chi)2^{s}\pi^{s-1}N^{1/2-s}\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(s+\delta)\right)\Gamma(1-s)L(1-s,\overline{\chi})\,.

(A.20)

where
\overline{\chi}
is the complex conjugate character of
\chi
, and

\displaystyle W(\chi)=\frac{\tau(\chi)}{{\rm i}^{\delta}\sqrt{N}}\,,

(A.21)

in terms of the Gauss sum

\displaystyle\tau(\chi)=\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(a)e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}a}{N}}\,.

(A.22)

With this, one can show that
L(s,\chi)
, for primitive
\chi
with modulus
N>1
, is an entire function of
s
.
One then also has
[
115
, Theorem 3.3.4]

\displaystyle L(k,\chi)=(-1)^{1+(k-\delta)/2}\frac{W(\chi)}{2\mathrm{i}^{\delta}}(2\pi/N)^{k}\frac{B_{k,\overline{\chi}}}{k!}\,,\quad L(1-k,\chi)=-B_{k,\chi}/k\,.

(A.23)

A.2
Eisenstein series with character

We summarize and extend some results about Eisenstein series from
[
115
, Chapter 7]
, see also
[
113
, Chapter 5]
.

Definition 1
.

Let
\chi\in D[N]
be a Dirichlet character with modulus
N\geq 1
and let
k>0
be such that
\chi(-1)=(-1)^{k}
.
We define the Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E_{k,\chi}(\tau):=

\displaystyle-\frac{B_{k}}{2k}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\chi(m/n)n^{k-1}q^{m}\,,

(A.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau):=

\displaystyle-\frac{B_{k,\chi}}{2k}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\chi(n)n^{k-1}q^{m}\,,

(A.25)

\displaystyle\widehat{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau):=

\displaystyle N^{\frac{k}{2}-1}\left(-\frac{B_{k}}{2k}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(-a)n^{k-1}e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}am}{nN}}q^{m}\right)\,.

(A.26)

We can then show the following:

Proposition 1
.

Let
\chi
and
k
be as in Definition
1
.

1.

If
N>1
or
k\neq 2
,
\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)
is a modular form of weight
k
for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
If
N=1
and
k=2
, it is the weight
2
quasimodular form
\widetilde{E}_{2,\chi_{1,0}}(\tau)=-\frac{1}{24}E_{2}(\tau)
.

2.

If
k\neq 2
or
\chi\neq\chi_{N,0}
,
E_{k,\chi}(\tau)
,
\widehat{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)
are modular forms of weight
k
and
E_{2,\chi_{N,0}}(\tau)
,
\widehat{E}_{2,\chi_{N,0}}(\tau)
are quasimodular forms of weight 2 for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.

Proof.

We first focus on
\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)
and assume that
k\neq 2
or
m_{\chi}>1
.
If
\chi
is primitive, the modular properties of
\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)
follow from
[
115
, Theorem 7.1.3]
,
[
115
, Theorem 7.2.12]
and
[
115
, Theorem 7.2.13]
, together with
[
115
, Lemma 7.1.1]
and
[
115
, Lemma 7.2.19]
.
If
\chi
is not primitive, we observe that for any function
f:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\chi(n)f(n,m)=&\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)\chi^{\star}(d)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\chi^{\star}(n)f(dn,dm)\,.\end{split}

(A.27)

Together with (
A.12
), this implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)=&-\frac{B_{k,\chi}}{2k}+\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)d^{k-1}\chi^{\star}(d)\left(\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi^{\star}}(d\tau)+\frac{B_{k,\chi^{\star}}}{2k}\right)\\
=&\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)d^{k-1}\chi^{\star}(d)\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi^{\star}}(d\tau)\,.\end{split}

(A.28)

We then observe that
\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi^{\star}}(\tau)
is a modular form of weight
k
for
\Gamma_{1}(m_{\chi})
and in the sum
\chi^{\star}(d)
is zero unless
d|N/m_{\chi}
.
If
k=2
and
\chi=\chi_{N,0}
, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{E}_{2,\chi}(\tau)=&-\frac{1}{24}\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)dE_{2}(d\tau)=-\frac{1}{24}\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)\left(E_{2}(\tau)+e_{d,2}(\tau)\right)\\
=&-\frac{1}{24}\left[\delta_{N,1}E_{2}(\tau)+\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)e_{d,2}(\tau)\right]\,,\end{split}

(A.29)

where in the last step we have used that
\sum_{d|N}\mu(d)=\delta_{1,N}
.

The modular properties of
E_{k,\chi}(\tau)
for
k\neq 2
or
m_{\chi}\neq 1
again follow from
[
115
, Theorem 7.1.3]
and
[
115
, Theorem 7.2.13]
, together with
[
115
, Lemma 7.1.1]
and
[
115
, Lemma 7.2.19]
.
For
k=2
,
\chi=\chi_{N,0}
we use again (
A.27
) and observe that

\displaystyle E_{2,\chi_{N,0}}(\tau)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{24}\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)E_{2}(d\tau)\,.

(A.30)

We can rewrite this again as

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{2,\chi_{N,0}}(\tau)=&-\frac{1}{24}\sum\limits_{d|N}\frac{\mu(d)}{d}\left(E_{2}(\tau)+e_{d,2}(\tau)\right)\\
=&-\frac{1}{24}\frac{\varphi(N)}{N}E_{2}(\tau)-\frac{1}{24}\sum\limits_{d|N}\frac{\mu(d)}{d}e_{d,2}(\tau)\,.\end{split}

(A.31)

where in the last step we have applied Möbius inversion to the divisor sum formula
\sum\limits_{d|N}\phi(d)=N
to obtain that
\sum_{d|N}\mu(d)/d=\phi(N)/N
.

The modular properties of
\widehat{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)
follow from Lemma
1
below.
∎

Lemma 1
.

With
\chi
,
k
as in Definition
1
and
L:=m_{\chi}
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)=\frac{N^{\frac{k}{2}}}{L}\chi(-1)W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)d^{-1}\chi^{\star}(d)E_{k,\overline{\chi}^{\star}}\left(\frac{N\tau}{Ld}\right)\,.

(A.32)

Proof.

We first calculate the non-constant part and define

\displaystyle f(\tau):=W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)d^{-1}\chi^{\star}(d)\left(E_{k,\overline{\chi}^{\star}}\left(\frac{N\tau}{Ld}\right)+\frac{B_{k}}{2k}\delta_{1,N}\right)\,.

(A.33)

Inserting the definition of
E_{k,\chi}(\tau)
, we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(\tau)=&W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)d^{-1}\chi^{\star}(d)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\overline{\chi}^{\star}(m/n)n^{k-1}q^{\frac{Nm}{Ld}}\\
=&W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{d|N/L}\mu(d)d^{-1}\chi^{\star}(d)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\overline{\chi}^{\star}(m/n)n^{k-1}q^{\frac{Nm}{Ld}}\\
=&\frac{L}{N}W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{d|N/L}d\mu\left(\frac{N}{Ld}\right)\chi^{\star}\left(\frac{N}{Ld}\right)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\overline{\chi}^{\star}(m/n)n^{k-1}q^{dm}\,,\end{split}

(A.34)

where in the second line we use that
\chi^{\star}(d)
can only be non-zero if
d|N/L
and in the third line we use
\sum_{d|N}f(d)=\sum_{d|N}f(N/d)
.
Exchanging the order of summation, and summing over
dm
instead of
m
, leads to the expression

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(\tau)=&\frac{L}{N}W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\sum\limits_{d|(N/L,m/n)}d\mu\left(\frac{N}{Ld}\right)\chi^{\star}\left(\frac{N}{Ld}\right)\overline{\chi}^{\star}\left(\frac{m}{dn}\right)n^{k-1}q^{m}\\
=&\frac{L}{N}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(a)n^{k-1}e^{2\pi{\rm i}am/(nN)}q^{m}\,,\\
\end{split}

(A.35)

where in the second line we have used
[
115
, Lemma 3.1.3]
.
To take care of the constant part, we just note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\chi(-1)W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{d|N}\frac{\mu(d)}{d}\chi^{\star}(d)\frac{B_{k}}{2k}\delta_{1,N}=-\frac{B_{k}}{2k}\delta_{1,N}\,.\end{split}

(A.36)

The result follows after comparing with (
A.26
).
∎

Lemma 2
.

Given
\chi\in D[N]
and
k\geq 1
, the (quasi) modular forms
\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)
and
\widehat{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)
are related via the Fricke involution

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}\right)=(\sqrt{N}\tau)^{k}\widehat{E}_{k,\chi}(\tau)+\delta_{N,1}\delta_{k,2}\frac{{\rm i}N}{4\pi}\tau\,.

(A.37)

Proof.

Let
L=m_{\chi}
be again the conductor of
\chi
.
We first assume that
k\geq 3
.
From
[
115
, Lemma 7.1.2]
one then obtains

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi^{\star}}\left(-\frac{1}{L\tau}\right)=\tau^{k}\frac{L^{k}}{W(\overline{\chi}^{\star})}E_{k,\overline{\chi}^{\star}}(\tau)\,,

(A.38)

where
L
is the conductor of the primitive character
\chi^{\star}
.
We also have
[
115
, Lemma 3.1.1]

\displaystyle W(\chi^{\star})W(\overline{\chi}^{\star})=\chi^{\star}(-1)L\,.

(A.39)

Together with (
A.28
), this gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{E}_{k,\chi}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}\right)=\tau^{k}\frac{N^{k}}{L}W(\chi^{\star})\sum\limits_{d|N}\mu(d)d^{-1}\chi^{\star}(-d)E_{k,\overline{\chi}^{\star}}\left(\frac{N\tau}{Ld}\right)\,.\end{split}

(A.40)

Together with Lemma
1
the claim follows.
The result can easily extended to
k=1,2
, using
[
115
, Theorem 7.2.13]
and
[
115
, Theorem 7.2.12]
.
∎

A.3
Polylogarithms and Eisenstein series with character

The (quasi) modular Eisenstein series
E_{k}(\tau)
for even
k\geq 2
can be expressed as

\displaystyle E_{2k-2}(\tau)=1-\frac{2(2k-2)}{B_{2k-2}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{3-2k}(q^{m})\,.

(A.41)

On the other hand, one also has

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}^{3}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{3}(q^{m})=\frac{1}{240}\left(E_{4}(\tau)-1\right)\,,\quad\partial_{\tau}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\text{Li}_{1}(q^{m})=\frac{1}{24}\left(1-E_{2}(\tau)\right)\,.

(A.42)

In this section we want to generalize these relations, using the Eisenstein series from Definition
1
.

First, recall that the indicator function
f_{N,a}(n)
is given by

\displaystyle f_{N,a}(n):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\,\,n\equiv a\text{ mod }N\\
0&\,\,n\not\equiv a\text{ mod }N\end{array}\right.\,,

and we also define
g_{N,a}(n):=f_{N,a}(n)+f_{N,-a}(n)
.

Our goal is to understand the modular properties of the following objects:

Definition 2
.

Given
a,N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\displaystyle\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau):=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}g_{N,a}(k){\text{Li}}_{3-2g}\left(q^{mN+k}\right)\,,

(A.45)

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau):=

\displaystyle N^{1-g}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}g_{N,a}(k){\text{Li}}_{3-2g}\left(e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}k}{N}}q^{m}\right)\,.

(A.46)

We can relate these to the Eisenstein series defined in Appendix
A.2
using the following lemma.

Lemma 3
.

Given
N,a\in\mathbb{N}
with
\gcd(N,a)=1
, one has

\displaystyle g_{N,a}(n)=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\frac{\chi(n)}{\chi(a)}\,.

(A.47)

Proof.

The indicator function can be written as

\displaystyle f_{N,a}(n)=\frac{1}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]}\chi(n)/\chi(a)\,,

(A.48)

which allows us to express
g_{N,a}(n)
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{N,a}(n)=&f_{N,a}(n)+f_{N,-a}(n)=\frac{1}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]}\left(1+\chi(-1))\right)\chi(n)/\chi(a)\,.\end{split}

(A.49)

The result then directly follows from the fact that
\chi(-1)\in\{-1,1\}
.
∎

Using Lemma
3
, it is easy to show the following:

Lemma 4
.

Given
N,a\in\mathbb{N}
with
r:=\gcd(N,a)
and
g\geq 2
, one has

\displaystyle\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2g-2}\delta_{1,N/r}+\frac{2}{\varphi(N/r)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N/r]^{+}}\frac{\chi(r)}{\chi(a)}E_{2g-2,\chi}(r\tau)\,,

(A.50)

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\frac{(N/r)^{1-g}B_{2g-2}}{2g-2}\delta_{1,N/r}+\frac{2N}{r\varphi(N/r)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N/r]^{+}}\frac{\chi(r)}{\chi(a)}\widehat{E}_{2g-2,\chi}(r\tau)\,.

(A.51)

Proof.

Let us first assume that
\gcd(a,N)=1
.
We can rewrite the Eisenstein series
E_{2g-2,\chi}(\tau)
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{2g-2,\chi}(\tau)=&-\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\chi(m/n)n^{2g-3}q^{m}\\
=&-\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}\chi(k)n^{2g-3}q^{n(mN+k)}\\
=&-\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\chi(k)\text{Li}_{3-2g}\left(q^{mN+k}\right)\,.\end{split}

(A.52)

Similarly, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{E}_{2g-2,\chi}(\tau)=&N^{g-2}\left(-\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(-a)n^{2g-3}e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}am}{nN}}q^{m}\right)\\
=&N^{g-2}\left(-\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m,n\geq 1}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(-a)n^{2g-3}e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}am}{N}}q^{mn}\right)\\
=&N^{g-2}\left(-\frac{B_{2g-2}}{2(2g-2)}\delta_{1,N}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N}\chi(-a)\text{Li}_{3-2g}\left(e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}a}{N}}q^{m}\right)\right)\,.\end{split}

(A.53)

In both cases the result then follows from Lemma
3
.
If
r=\gcd(a,N)\neq 1
we have
\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}=\phi^{(g)}_{N/r,a/r}(r\tau)
and
\widehat{\phi}^{(g)}_{N,a}=\widehat{\phi}^{(g)}_{N/r,a/r}(r\tau)
.
∎

Lemma 5
.

Given
N,a\in\mathbb{N}
with
r:=\gcd(a,N)
and
g\in\{0,1\}
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)\\
=&\frac{\beta_{4-2g,N/r,a/r}}{4-2g}+\frac{2}{\varphi(N/r)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N/r]^{+}}\frac{\chi(r)}{\chi(a)}\widetilde{E}_{4-2g,\chi}(r\tau)\,,\\
&(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\widehat{\phi}^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)\\
=&\left(\frac{N}{r}\right)^{1-g}\frac{B_{4-2g}}{4-2g}+\frac{2}{\varphi(N/r)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N/r]^{+}}\frac{\chi(r)}{\chi(a)}\widehat{E}_{4-2g,\chi}(r\tau)\,.\end{split}

(A.54)

Proof.

We first rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)=&\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}g_{N,a}(k)(mN+k)^{3-2g}\text{Li}_{0}(q^{mN+k})\\
=&\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}g_{N,a}(n)n^{3-2g}q^{m}\,.\end{split}

(A.55)

Using Definition
1
, this can be expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)\\
=&\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})\left(\widetilde{E}_{4-2g,\chi}(\tau)+\frac{B_{4-2g,\chi}}{2(4-2g)}\right)\\
=&\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})\left[\widetilde{E}_{4-2g,\chi}(\tau)+\frac{1}{2(4-2g)}\sum\limits_{b=1}^{N}\chi(b)\beta_{4-2g,N,b}\right]\\
=&\frac{\beta_{4-2g,N,a}}{4-2g}+\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})\widetilde{E}_{4-2g,\chi}(\tau)\,.\end{split}

(A.56)

Similarly, we first rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\widehat{\phi}^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)=&N^{1-g}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{b=1}^{N}g_{N,a}(b)m^{3-2g}\text{Li}_{0}\left(e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}b}{N}}q^{m}\right)\\
=&N^{1-g}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}\sum\limits_{b=1}^{N}g_{N,a}(b)m^{3-2g}e^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}nb}{N}}q^{nm}\,.\end{split}

(A.57)

Using again Definition
1
, this can be expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}&\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\widehat{\phi}^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)\\
=&\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})\left(\widehat{E}_{4-2g,\chi}(\tau)+N^{1-g}\frac{B_{4-2g}}{2(4-2g)}\varphi(N)\delta_{m_{\chi},1}\right)\\
=&N^{1-g}\frac{B_{4-2g}}{4-2g}+\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})\widehat{E}_{4-2g,\chi}(\tau)\,.\end{split}

(A.58)

∎

A.4
Eichler integrals

In this appendix we generalize the relations (
3.12
) and (
3.15
) and derive the modular properties of the functions
\phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)
for
g=0,1
.

Let us first make the following definitions.

Definition 3
.

Given
g\in\{0,1\}
and
N,a\in\mathbb{N}
we define

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau):=&c^{(g)}_{N,a}\tau^{3-2g}+\phi_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(\tau\right)\,,\\
\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau):=&\hat{c}^{(g)}_{N}\tau^{3-2g}+\widehat{\phi}_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(\tau\right)\,,\end{split}

(A.59)

with

\displaystyle c^{(g)}_{N,a}:=-\frac{(2\pi{\rm i})^{3-2g}\beta_{4-2g,N,a}}{(4-2g)[(3-2g)!]}\,,\quad\hat{c}^{(g)}_{N}:=-\frac{(2\pi{\rm i})^{3-2g}N^{1-g}B_{4-2g}}{(4-2g)[(3-2g)!]}\,,

(A.60)

as well as

\displaystyle\varphi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau):=(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\Phi^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)\,,\quad\widehat{\varphi}_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau):=(2\pi{\rm i})^{2g-3}\partial_{\tau}^{3-2g}\widehat{\Phi}^{(g)}_{N,a}(\tau)\,.

(A.61)

Note that
\Phi_{1,1}^{(0)}(\tau)=\widehat{\Phi}_{1,1}^{(0)}(\tau)=2\widetilde{E}_{-2}(\tau)-\zeta(3)
and
\Phi_{1,1}^{(1)}(\tau)=\widehat{\Phi}_{1,1}^{(1)}(\tau)=-2\widetilde{E}_{0}(\tau)
.

From Lemma
5
, we know that both
\varphi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
and
\widehat{\varphi}_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
are (quasi) modular forms of weight
4-2g
for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
.
Moreover, Lemma
2
implies that they are related via the Fricke involution

\displaystyle\varphi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)=(\sqrt{N}\tau)^{2g-4}\widehat{\varphi}_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}\right)+\delta_{g,1}\delta_{N,1}\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}\tau}\,.

(A.62)

Our goal in this section is to study the transformation behavior under this Fricke involution of
\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
and
\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
and to use this to deduce the modular properties of
\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
.
More precisely, we want to show the following:

Theorem 1
.

Given
N,a\in\mathbb{N}
, and
r:=\gcd(N,a)
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\tau+1\right)=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\tau\right)+c^{(0)}_{N,a}(3\tau^{2}+3\tau+1)\,,\\
(N\tau+1)^{2}\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\frac{\tau}{N\tau+1}\right)=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\tau\right)-\frac{{\rm i}\pi^{3}}{90}\left[2(15a^{2}-15aN+2N^{2})\tau^{2}-3N\tau-3\right]\\
&-\zeta(3)\tau(\tau+2)\delta_{1,N/r}\,,\end{split}

as well as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(\tau+1\right)=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(\tau\right)+c^{(1)}_{N,a}\,,\\
\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(\frac{\tau}{N\tau+1}\right)=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(\tau\right)-\delta_{1,N/r}\log\left(N\tau+1\right)-\hat{c}^{(1)}_{N,a}\,.\end{split}

Proof.

The behavior under
\tau\mapsto\tau+1
is trivial.
Deriving the transformation under
\tau\rightarrow\tau/(N\tau+1)
will occupy us for the rest of this section.

If
\gcd(N,a)=r
, with
r>1
, then
\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\tau\right)=\Phi_{N^{\prime},a^{\prime}}^{(0)}\left(r\tau\right)
, with
N^{\prime}=N/r
and
a^{\prime}=a/r
.
We can therefore assume that
\gcd(a,N)=1
and the general result follows as well.

Since
\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
and
\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
are Eichler integrals of the modular forms
\varphi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
and
\widehat{\varphi}_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)
, we know that

\displaystyle\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)-(\sqrt{N}\tau)^{2-2g}\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}\right)=P_{N,a}^{(g)}(\tau)\,,

(A.63)

in terms of the functions

\displaystyle P_{N,a}^{(0)}(\tau)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2}\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,k}\tau^{k}\,,\quad P_{N,a}^{(1)}(\tau)=\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}\log(\tau)+\alpha^{(1)}_{N,a,0}\,,

(A.64)

with complex coefficients
\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}
,
\alpha^{(1)}_{N,a,0}
and
\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,k}
for
k=0,1,2
.
This also implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(\tau\right)-(\sqrt{N}\tau)^{2-2g}\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}\right)=&-(\sqrt{N}\tau)^{2-2g}P_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}\right)\,.\end{split}

(A.65)

Following the strategy laid out in
[
89
]
, we define

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{N,a}^{(g)}(y)=&(-1)^{g}{\rm i}c^{(g)}_{N,a}y^{3-2g}+\Phi_{N,a}^{(g)}({\rm i}y)=\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})g_{N,\chi}^{(g)}(y)\,,\\
\hat{g}_{N,a}^{(g)}(y)=&(-1)^{g}{\rm i}\hat{c}^{(g)}_{N}y^{3-2g}+\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(g)}({\rm i}y)=\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})\hat{g}_{N,\chi}^{(g)}(y)\,,\end{split}

(A.66)

in terms of

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{N,\chi}^{(g)}(y)=&\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\chi(k)\text{Li}_{3-2g}\left(e^{-2\pi(mN+k)y}\right)\,,\\
\hat{g}_{N,\chi}^{(g)}(y)=&N^{1-g}\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\chi(k)\text{Li}_{3-2g}\left(e^{2\pi{\rm i}k/N}e^{-2\pi my}\right)\,.\end{split}

(A.67)

From (
A.63
) we then obtain

\displaystyle g_{N,a}^{(g)}(y)=(-1)^{1-g}(\sqrt{N}y)^{2-2g}\hat{g}_{N,a}^{(g)}\left(\frac{1}{Ny}\right)+R^{(g)}_{N,a}(y)\,,

(A.68)

in terms of

\displaystyle R^{(0)}_{N,a}(y)=\sum\limits_{k=-1}^{3}A^{(0)}_{N,a,k}y^{k}\,,\quad R^{(1)}_{N,a}(y)=\widetilde{A}^{(1)}_{N,a}\log(y)+\sum\limits_{k=-1}^{1}A^{(1)}_{N,a,k}y^{k}\,,

(A.69)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}A^{(0)}_{N,a,-1}=&{\rm i}\hat{c}^{(0)}_{N}/N^{2}\,,\quad A^{(0)}_{N,a,0}=\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,0}\,,\quad A^{(0)}_{N,a,1}={\rm i}\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,1}\,,\\
A^{(0)}_{N,a,2}=&-\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,2}\,,\quad A^{(0)}_{N,a,3}={\rm i}c^{(0)}_{N,a}\,,\end{split}

(A.70)

as well as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{A}^{(1)}_{N,a}=&\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}\,,\quad A^{(1)}_{N,a,-1}={\rm i}\hat{c}^{(1)}_{N}/N\,,\\
A^{(1)}_{N,a,0}=&\alpha^{(1)}_{N,a,0}+\frac{1}{2}\pi{\rm i}\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}\,,\quad A^{(1)}_{N,a,1}=-{\rm i}c^{(1)}_{N,a}\,.\end{split}

(A.71)

In order to determine the coefficients
A^{(g)}_{N,a,k}
, we calculate the poles of the Mellin transform

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{N,\chi}^{(g)}(s)=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}y^{s-1}g_{N,\chi}^{(g)}(y)dy\,.

(A.72)

More precisely, if we denote the residue of
\tilde{g}^{(g)}_{N,\chi}(s)
at
s=-k
by
A^{(g)}_{N,\chi,k}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{N,a,k}^{(g)}=\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})A^{(g)}_{N,\chi,k}\,,

(A.73)

After rewriting

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{N,\chi}^{(g)}(y)=&\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}\chi(k)\frac{e^{-2\pi n(mN+k)y}}{n^{3-2g}}=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\frac{\chi(m/n)}{n^{3-2g}}e^{-2\pi my}\,,\end{split}

(A.74)

we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}(2\pi)^{s}\tilde{g}^{(g)}_{N,\chi}(s)=&(2\pi)^{s}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\frac{\chi(m/n)}{n^{3-2g}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}y^{s-1}e^{-2\pi my}dy\\
=&\Gamma(s)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n|m}\frac{\chi(m/n)}{n^{3-2g+s}(m/n)^{s}}=\Gamma(s)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}\frac{\chi(n)}{n^{3-2g+s}(n)^{s}}\\
=&\Gamma(s)\zeta(3-2g+s)L(s,\chi)\,.\end{split}

(A.75)

Using the relation (
A.17
), we can further rewrite this as

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{(g)}_{N,\chi}(s)=(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma(s)\zeta(3-2g+s)L(s,\chi^{\star})\prod\limits_{p|N}\left(1-\frac{\chi^{\star}(p)}{p^{s}}\right)\,.

(A.76)

If the conductor of
\chi
is greater than one, the Dirichlet L-series
L(s,\chi^{\star})
is an entire function of
s
.
Otherwise, we have
\chi=\chi_{N,0}
and

\displaystyle L(s,\chi_{N,0})=\zeta(s)\prod\limits_{p|N}\left(1-p^{-s}\right)\,.

(A.77)

Recall that the only pole of
\zeta(s)
is a simple pole at
s=1
with residue
1
and the zeros on the real axis lie at the even negative integers.
The gamma function
\Gamma(s)
has simple poles at values
s=-n
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with residue

\displaystyle\text{Res}(\Gamma,-n)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\,.

(A.78)

Case
g=0
.

We first consider
g=0
. Then
\tilde{g}^{(g)}_{N,\chi}(s)
has potential (simple) poles at
s\in\{-3,\ldots,1\}
.
One immediately gets

\displaystyle A^{(0)}_{N,\chi,3}=\frac{4}{6}\pi^{3}L(-3,\chi)\,,\quad A^{(0)}_{N,\chi,1}=-\frac{1}{3}\pi^{3}L(-1,\chi)\,,\quad A^{(0)}_{N,\chi,0}=\zeta(3)L(0,\chi)\,,

(A.79)

and, using (
A.77
),

\displaystyle A^{(0)}_{N,\chi,-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if }\chi\neq\chi_{N,0}\\
\frac{\pi^{3}}{180}\prod\limits_{p|N}\left(1-p^{-1}\right)&\text{ if }\chi=\chi_{N,0}\end{array}\right.\,.

To obtain
A^{(0)}_{N,\chi,2}
, we first use (
A.20
) and note that for primitive
\chi\in D[N]^{+}
one has

\displaystyle L(\epsilon-2,\chi)=-\frac{N^{\frac{5}{2}}W(\chi)}{4\pi^{2}}L(3,\overline{\chi})\epsilon+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\,,

(A.82)

such that

\displaystyle A^{(0)}_{N,\chi,2}=-\frac{1}{2}m_{\chi}^{\frac{5}{2}}W(\chi)L(3,\overline{\chi})\,.

(A.83)

Using the relation (
A.73
), as well as (
A.1
), we then obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}A^{(0)}_{N,a,3}=&-\frac{\pi^{3}}{3}\beta_{4,N,a}\,,\quad A^{(0)}_{N,a,1}=\frac{\pi^{3}}{3}\beta_{2,N,a}\,,\\
A^{(0)}_{N,a,0}=&-\zeta(3)\delta_{1,N}\,,\quad A^{(0)}_{N,a,-1}=\frac{\pi^{3}}{90N}\,,\end{split}

(A.84)

and this fixes

\displaystyle\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,0}=-\frac{1}{2}\zeta(3)\delta_{1,N}\,,\quad\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,1}=-{\rm i}\frac{\pi^{3}}{3}\beta_{2,N,a}\,.

(A.85)

We can now use these results to calculate

\displaystyle\begin{split}(N\tau+1)^{2}&\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\frac{\tau}{N\tau+1}\right)=(N\tau+1)^{2}\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}-1\right)}\right)\\
=&(\sqrt{N}\tau)^{2}\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}-1\right)+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2}\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,k}\tau^{k}(N\tau+1)^{2-k}\\
=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\tau\right)-\frac{\hat{c}^{(0)}_{N}}{N}\left[3+3N\tau+N^{2}\tau^{2}\right]+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2}\alpha^{(0)}_{N,a,k}\tau^{k}\left[(N\tau+1)^{2-k}-1\right]\\
=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(0)}\left(\tau\right)-\frac{{\rm i}\pi^{3}}{90}\left[2(15a^{2}-15aN+2N^{2})\tau^{2}-3N\tau-3\right]-\zeta(3)\tau(\tau+2)\delta_{1,N}\,.\end{split}

(A.86)

Case
g=1
.

The case
g=1
works analogously and
\tilde{g}^{(1)}_{N,\chi}(s)
has simple poles
s\in\{-1,1\}
and a potential double pole at
s=0
.
We immediately obtain the residues

\displaystyle A^{(1)}_{N,\chi,1}=\pi L(-1,\chi)\,,\quad A^{(1)}_{N,\chi,-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if }m_{\chi}\neq 1\\
\frac{\pi}{12}\prod\limits_{p|N}\left(1-p^{-1}\right)&\text{ if }m_{\chi}=1\end{array}\right.\,,

such that

\displaystyle A^{(1)}_{N,a,1}=-\pi\beta_{2,N,a}\,,\quad A^{(1)}_{N,a,-1}=\frac{\pi}{6N}\,.

(A.89)

Around the double pole, we expand

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{(1)}_{N,\chi}(s)=\frac{1}{s^{2}}\widetilde{A}^{(1)}_{N,\chi}+\frac{1}{s}A^{(1)}_{N,\chi,0}+\mathcal{O}(1)\,,

(A.90)

and, after comparing with (
A.76
), we find that

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}^{(1)}_{N,\chi}=\widetilde{A}^{(1)}_{N,\chi}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if }\,N>1\\
-\frac{1}{2}&\text{ if }\,N=1\end{array}\right.\,,

where we have used (
A.1
).
Since for
s>0
one has

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{0}\log(y)y^{s-1}dy=-\frac{1}{s^{2}}\,,

(A.93)

we can identify

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{N,a}^{(1)}=-\frac{2}{\varphi(N)}\sum\limits_{\chi\in D[N]^{+}}\chi(a^{-1})\widetilde{A}^{(1)}_{N,\chi}\,.

(A.94)

It follows that

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{N,a}^{(1)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if }\,N>1\\
1&\text{ if }\,N=a=1\end{array}\right.\,.

We skip the calculation of
A^{(1)}_{N,\chi,0}
as it won’t be necessary for our purpose.

We can now use these results to calculate

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}&\left(\frac{\tau}{N\tau+1}\right)\\
=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}-1\right)}\right)\\
=&\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}-1\right)+\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}\log\left(\frac{\tau}{N\tau+1}\right)-\alpha^{(1)}_{N,a,0}\\
=&\widehat{\Phi}_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(-\frac{1}{N\tau}\right)-\hat{c}^{(1)}_{N}+\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}\log\left(\frac{\tau}{N\tau+1}\right)-\alpha^{(1)}_{N,a,0}\\
=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(\tau\right)-\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}\log\left(\tau\right)+\tilde{\alpha}_{N,a}^{(1)}\log\left(\frac{\tau}{N\tau+1}\right)-\hat{c}^{(1)}_{N}\\
=&\Phi_{N,a}^{(1)}\left(\tau\right)-\delta_{N,1}\log\left(N\tau+1\right)-\hat{c}^{(1)}_{N}\,.\end{split}

(A.97)

∎

A.4.1
Extra generators

Here, we record conjectural identities for the transformation of the genus 0 and genus 1 Eichler integrals under the additional generators
\scriptsize\begin{pmatrix}11&-4\\
25&-9\end{pmatrix}
for
N=5
and
\scriptsize\begin{pmatrix}7&-3\\
12&-5\end{pmatrix}
for
N=6
, respectively. While these identities have been checked numerically, we have not attempted to prove them:

\begin{split}(25\tau-9)^{2}\Phi_{5,0}^{(0)}\left(\frac{11\tau-4}{25\tau-9}\right)-\Phi_{5,0}^{(0)}(\tau)=&\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{144}\left(-123+635\tau-800\tau^{2}\right)\\
(25\tau-9)^{2}\Phi_{5,1}^{(0)}\left(\frac{11\tau-4}{25\tau-9}\right)-\Phi_{5,1}^{(0)}(\tau)=&\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{720}\left(-399+2263\tau-3232\tau^{2}\right)\\
(25\tau-9)^{2}\Phi_{5,2}^{(0)}\left(\frac{11\tau-4}{25\tau-9}\right)-\Phi_{5,2}^{(0)}(\tau)=&\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{720}\left(681-3737\tau+5108\tau^{2}\right)\\
(12\tau-5)^{2}\Phi_{6,0}^{(0)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,0}^{(0)}(\tau)=&-\frac{3(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{400}\left(984\tau^{2}-888\tau+197\right)\\
(12\tau-5)^{2}\Phi_{6,1}^{(0)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,1}^{(0)}(\tau)=&\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{1600}\left(-5781\tau^{2}+5207\tau-1153\right)\\
(12\tau-5)^{2}\Phi_{6,2}^{(0)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,2}^{(0)}(\tau)=&\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{450}\left(333-1502\tau+1666\tau^{2}\right)\\
(12\tau-5)^{2}\Phi_{6,3}^{(0)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,3}^{(0)}(\tau)=&\frac{27(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{6400}\left(341-1539\tau+1707\tau^{2}\right)\end{split}

(A.98)

\begin{split}\Phi_{5,0}^{(1)}\left(\frac{11\tau-4}{25\tau-9}\right)-\Phi_{5,0}^{(1)}(\tau)=&\frac{5}{12}2\pi\mathrm{i}-\log(25\tau-9)\\
\Phi_{5,1}^{(1)}\left(\frac{11\tau-4}{25\tau-9}\right)-\Phi_{5,1}^{(1)}(\tau)=&-\frac{11}{60}2\pi\mathrm{i}\\
\Phi_{5,2}^{(1)}\left(\frac{11\tau-4}{25\tau-9}\right)-\Phi_{5,2}^{(1)}(\tau)=&\frac{1}{60}2\pi\mathrm{i}\\
\Phi_{6,0}^{(1)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,0}^{(1)}(\tau)=&\frac{1}{6}2\pi\mathrm{i}-\log(12\tau-5)\\
\Phi_{6,1}^{(1)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,1}^{(1)}(\tau)=&-\frac{1}{12}2\pi\mathrm{i}\\
\Phi_{6,2}^{(1)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,2}^{(1)}(\tau)=&\frac{1}{6}2\pi\mathrm{i}\\
\Phi_{6,3}^{(1)}\left(\frac{7\tau-3}{12\tau-5}\right)-\Phi_{6,3}^{(1)}(\tau)=&-\frac{1}{12}2\pi\mathrm{i}\end{split}

(A.99)

Appendix B
Relative conifold monodromy

In this section, we derive the action (
4.2
) of the relative conifold monodromy on the Chern characters of a basis of branes.
We use the same notation as in the main text, but distinguish the quantities

a_{\alpha}=c_{1}(T_{B})\cap\check{D}_{\alpha}\,,\quad c_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{12}c_{2}(T_{X})\cap D_{\alpha}\,,\quad\alpha=1,\ldots,b_{2}(B)\,.

(B.1)

since we cannot prove that they are equal in general.

Let us denote the two projections from
X\times_{B}X
to
X
by
\pi_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
As discussed in
[
33
, Section 3.3]
, the relative conifold monodromy
U
acts on the Chern character of a brane
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X)
as

\displaystyle U:\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})\mapsto\text{ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})-\pi_{2,*}\left[\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\text{ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\right)\right]\,,

(B.2)

where
T_{X/B}
is the virtual relative tangent bundle of the fibration.
The Chern class of the virtual relative tangent bundle
T_{X/B}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}c(T_{X/B})=&\frac{1+c_{1}(T_{X})+c_{2}(T_{X})+c_{3}(T_{X})}{1+c_{1}(T_{B})+c_{2}(T_{B})}\\
=&1-c_{1}(T_{B})+c_{1}(T_{B})^{2}-c_{2}(T_{B})+c_{2}(T_{X})-c_{1}(T_{B})c_{2}(T_{X})+c_{3}(T_{X})\,.\end{split}

(B.3)

and the corresponding Todd genus takes the form

\displaystyle\text{Td}(T_{X/B})=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{c_{1}(T_{B})}{2}+\frac{2c_{1}(T_{B})^{2}-c_{2}(T_{B})+c_{2}(T_{X})}{12}-\frac{c_{1}(T_{B})c_{2}(T_{X})}{24}\,.

(B.4)

A
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of the cohomology of
X
is given by the classes

\displaystyle\{\,1,\,D_{e},\,D_{\alpha},\,F,\,D_{e}D_{\alpha},\,V\,\}\,,

(B.5)

where
F
is the 4-form that is Poincaré dual to the generic fiber and
V
is the volume 6-form that is dual to a point on
X
.
They satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{e}^{2}=&\frac{1}{N}\left(\kappa-\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}\ell_{\beta}C^{\alpha\beta}\right)F+\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}D_{\beta}D_{e}\,,\quad D_{\alpha}D_{\beta}=C_{\alpha\beta}F\,,\\
D_{e}^{3}=&\kappa V\,,\quad D_{e}^{2}D_{\alpha}=\ell_{\alpha}V\,,\quad D_{e}D_{\alpha}D_{\beta}=NC_{\alpha\beta}V\,,\quad D_{\alpha}D_{\beta}D_{\gamma}=0\,,\\
FD_{e}=&NV\,,\quad FD_{\alpha}=0\,.\end{split}

(B.6)

We can interpret
\pi_{2,*}\pi_{1}^{*}
as an endomorphism of
H^{\bullet}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, where it acts as

\displaystyle 1\mapsto 0\,,\quad D_{\alpha}\mapsto 0\,,\quad F\mapsto 0\,,\quad D_{e}\mapsto N\,,\quad D_{e}D_{\alpha}\mapsto ND_{\alpha}\,,\quad V\mapsto F\,.

(B.7)

The base
B
is assumed to be a generalized del Pezzo surface and therefore rational.
This implies that the only non-vanishing Hodge numbers are
h^{1,1}(B)=b_{2}(B)
and
h^{0,0}(B)=h^{2,2}(B)=1
.
Then
c_{2}(T_{B})=(2+b_{2}(B))F
and Noether’s formula, together with the fact that the holomorphic Euler characteristic of a rational surface is
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{B})=1
, implies that
c_{1}(T_{B})^{2}=a_{\alpha}a_{\beta}C^{\alpha\beta}=(10-b_{2}(B))F
.

As a result, we can rewrite the Todd genus as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Td}(T_{X/B})=&1-\frac{1}{2}a^{\alpha}D_{\alpha}+\left(\frac{1}{4}a_{\alpha}a_{\beta}C^{\alpha\beta}-1\right)F+\frac{1}{12}c_{2}(TX)-\frac{1}{2}c_{\alpha}a^{\alpha}V\,.\end{split}

(B.8)

By demanding that
c_{2}(TX)\cap(D_{e},D_{\alpha})=(c_{2,e},12c_{\alpha})
, we also obtain that

\displaystyle c_{2}(T_{X})=\frac{12}{N}c_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}D_{\beta}D_{e}+\frac{1}{N}\left(c_{2,e}-\frac{12}{N}c_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}\ell_{\beta}\right)F\,.

(B.9)

D6-brane:

The Chern character of the structure sheaf is
\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=1
.
We then obtain

\displaystyle\pi_{2,*}\left[\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\right)\right]=c_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}D_{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}c_{\alpha}a^{\alpha}F\,.

(B.10)

The Chern character of a brane
\mathcal{O}_{D}
that is supported on a Cartier divisor
D
is given by

\displaystyle\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D})=1-\exp(-D)=D-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}+\frac{1}{6}D^{3}\,.

(B.11)

We can therefore express the right-hand side of (
B.10
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&c_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\beta}})+\frac{1}{2}c_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}D_{\beta}\left(D_{\beta}-c_{1}(T_{B})\right)\\
=&c_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\beta}})+\frac{1}{2}c_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta}\left(c_{\beta\beta}-a_{\beta}\right)F\,.\end{split}

(B.12)

D4-brane on
D_{e}
:

The Chern character of a brane
\mathcal{O}_{D_{e}}
that is supported on the
N
-section
D_{e}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{e}})=&1-\exp(-{D_{e}})\\
=&S-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}D_{e}+\frac{1}{N}\left(\kappa-\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}\ell_{\beta}C^{\alpha\beta}\right)F\right]+\frac{1}{6}\kappa V\,,\end{split}

(B.13)

such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{e}})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\\
=&D_{e}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(a_{\alpha}+\ell_{\alpha}\right)C^{\alpha\beta}D_{\beta}D_{e}+\frac{1}{N}\left(\kappa-\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}\ell_{\beta}C^{\alpha\beta}\right)F\right]\\
&+\left[\frac{N}{4}\left(6-b_{2}(B)\right)+\frac{1}{12}c_{2,e}+\frac{1}{4}\ell_{\alpha}a^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{6}\kappa\right]V\,.\end{split}

(B.14)

Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\pi_{2,*}\left[\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{e}})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\right)\right]\\
=&N-\frac{N}{2}\left(a_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}\right)C^{\alpha\beta}D_{\beta}+\left[\frac{N}{4}\left(6-b_{2}(B)\right)+\frac{1}{12}c_{2,e}+\frac{1}{4}\ell_{\alpha}a^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{6}\kappa\right]F\\
=&N\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{X})-\frac{N}{2}\left(a_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}\right)C^{\alpha\beta}\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\beta}})\\
&+\left[-\frac{N}{4}\left(a_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\alpha}\right)C^{\alpha\beta}C_{\beta\beta}+\frac{N}{4}\left(6-b_{2}(B)\right)+\frac{1}{12}c_{2,e}+\frac{1}{4}\ell_{\alpha}a^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{6}\kappa\right]F\,.\end{split}

D4-brane on
D_{\alpha}
:

The Chern character of a brane
\mathcal{O}_{D_{\alpha}}
that is supported on the vertical divisor
D_{\alpha}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\alpha}})=&1-\exp(-D_{\alpha})=D_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}c_{\alpha\alpha}F\,.\end{split}

(B.15)

The relevant terms of the product with the Todd genus are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\alpha}})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})=&\frac{1}{N}c_{\gamma}C^{\gamma\beta}D_{\beta}D_{\alpha}S+\ldots\,,\end{split}

(B.16)

such that

\displaystyle\pi_{2,*}\left[\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\alpha}})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\right)\right]=c_{\gamma}C^{\gamma\beta}C_{\beta\alpha}F=c_{\alpha}F\,.

(B.17)

D2-brane on
C^{\alpha}
:

The relevant terms of the product with the Todd genus are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{ch}(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})=&C^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}a^{\beta}D_{\beta}C^{\alpha}+\ldots=C^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}a^{\alpha}V+\ldots\,,\end{split}

(B.18)

such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi_{2,*}&\left[\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\text{ch}(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\right)\right]\\
=&D^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}a^{\alpha}F=C^{\alpha\beta}\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\beta}})+\frac{1}{2}C^{\alpha\beta}(C_{\beta\beta}-a_{\beta})F\,.\end{split}

(B.19)

D2-brane on
F
:

We have

\displaystyle\pi_{2,*}\left[\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\right)\right]=0\,.

(B.20)

D0-brane:

We have

\displaystyle\pi_{2,*}\left[\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{\text{pt.}})\text{Td}(T_{X/B})\right)\right]=F\,.

(B.21)

Result

We choose the following basis of the charge lattice

\displaystyle\left\{\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{e}}),\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{\alpha}}),\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}),\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}),\,\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{\text{pt.}})\,\right\}\,,

(B.22)

where
\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}})=\frac{1}{N}\text{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
.
The end result then takes the form

\displaystyle U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1&0&-c^{\beta}&\frac{N}{2}c^{\gamma}(a_{\gamma}-C_{\gamma\gamma})&0_{\beta}&0\\
-N&1&\frac{N}{2}\left(a_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{N}\ell_{\gamma}\right)C^{\gamma\beta}&\rho&0_{\beta}&0\\
0_{\alpha}&0_{\alpha}&\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha}&-Nc_{\alpha}&0_{\alpha\beta}&0_{\alpha}\\
0&0&0^{\beta}&1&0_{\beta}&0\\
0^{\alpha}&0^{\alpha}&-C^{\alpha\beta}&\frac{N}{2}C^{\alpha\gamma}\left(a_{\gamma}-C_{\gamma\gamma}\right)&\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}&0^{\alpha}\\
0&0&0^{\beta}&-N&0_{\beta}&1\end{array}\right)\,,

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho=&N^{2}+\frac{N}{4}(\ell_{\alpha}+Na_{\alpha})C^{\alpha\beta}(C_{\beta\beta}-a_{\beta})-\frac{N}{12}(2\kappa+c_{2,e})\,.\end{split}

(B.29)

If the curves in the classes
C_{\alpha}
are rational one can use that
c_{\alpha\alpha}-a_{\alpha}=-2
to obtain the expression

\displaystyle\rho=-N\left[\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=1}^{b_{2}(B)}(Na^{\beta}+\ell_{\alpha}C^{\alpha\beta})-N+\frac{1}{6}\kappa\right]+\frac{1}{12}c_{2,e}\,.

(B.30)

Appendix C
Genus one fibrations

In this section we will discuss the construction of genus one fibered CY threefolds
\pi:X\rightarrow B
that exhibit an
N
-section for
N=1,\ldots 5
.
We assume that the base
B
is a generalized del Pezzo surface and that
X
is smooth with
b_{2}(X)=b_{2}(B)+1
.

For
N=1
, i.e. when the fibration admits a section, the topology of the fibration is uniquely determined by the choice of basis
B
.
It can be realized as a fibration of Weierstraß curves, that is a hypersurface

\displaystyle\{\,y^{2}=x^{3}+fxz^{4}+gz^{6}\,\}\subset\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{2}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{3}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{B}\right)\,,

(C.1)

where the CY condition requires
\mathcal{L}
to be the anti-canonical bundle on
B
and the Weierstraß coefficients
f,g
are respectively sections of
\mathcal{L}^{4}
and
\mathcal{L}^{6}
.
All of the fibers of
X
are irreducible and the topological invariants are listed in Table
3
.

The situation becomes more interesting for fibrations that only admit an
N
-section with
N\geq 2
.
The fibration will then exhibit isolated
I_{2}
-fibers, where the torus degenerates into two rational curves that intersect in two points.
Since the
N
-section by definition intersects the generic smooth fiber
N
times, it intersects the two rational curves of a given
I_{2}
-fiber respectively
q
and
N-q
times for some
1\leq q\leq\lfloor{\tiny\frac{N}{2}}\rfloor
.
30
30

30

Strictly speaking,
q
could be negative if the
N
-section degenerates and wraps a 1-dimensional component of the
I_{2}
-fiber. However, we don’t encounter this situation for
2\leq N\leq 5
.

We will denote the number of such
I_{2}
-fibers by
n_{\pm q}\in\mathbb{N}
.
It is related to the multiplicity
N_{k}
of fibral curves intersecting the
N
-section
k
times, that has been introduced in Section
2.4
, via
n_{\pm q}=\frac{1}{2}(N_{q}+N_{N-q})
.
The topology of the fibration then depends not only on the base
B
, but also on the numbers of
I_{2}
-fibers
n_{\pm q}
for
1\leq q\leq\lfloor{\tiny\frac{N}{2}}\rfloor
and, as was observed in
[
41
]
for
N=3
, on the height-pairing of the
N
-section.

As we will describe below, for
N\geq 2
the fibration can always be realized as a double cover (for
N=2
) or a subvariety of a
\mathbb{P}^{N-1}
-bundle on
B
.
For
2\leq N\leq 4
, this follows from a fiberwise application of the constructions summarized in
[
116
]
, for
N=5
from the construction discussed in
[
117
]
and for
N\geq 6
from the results from
[
118
]
.
For
2\leq N\leq 5
we will derive closed expressions for the topological invariants of the fibration in terms of the invariants of a set of vector bundles that appear in this construction.
The resulting expressions are also listed in Table
3
.

\left.+\Delta(V)+\Delta(E)\right]
